---

# 1. Phase 1 — High-Fidelity State Mapping

> "Every engine begins with a question: what is the *state*? Get this wrong, and no amount of algorithmic cleverness or hardware optimization will save you. Get it right, and the rest of the engineering is downhill. Phase 1 of the engine lifecycle is the most important phase, and it is the phase most engineers rush through."

This is the first of five notes covering the practical engineering lifecycle of building an engine. The lifecycle is divided into five phases:

1. **Phase 1: High-fidelity state mapping.** Define the state $S$ and context $C$.
2. **Phase 2: Build the transition function $F$.**
3. **Phase 3: Structural optimizations over brute force.**
4. **Phase 4: Profiling-driven micro-optimization.**
5. **Phase 5: Event loop orchestration.**

Each phase builds on the previous. Skipping or rushing a phase leads to problems in later phases that are expensive to fix.

---

## 6.1.1 Abstracting and Minimizing System Representation

The first task of Phase 1 is to **abstract** the real-world problem into a tractable computational representation. The real world is messy; the engine's state must be clean.

### The Abstraction Process

Abstraction proceeds in three steps:

1. **Identify the entities.** What are the "things" in the problem? (Chess: pieces, squares, side to move. Trading: orders, prices, positions. Search: documents, terms, queries.)
2. **Identify the relationships.** How do the entities relate? (Chess: pieces occupy squares; one side moves at a time. Trading: orders have prices and quantities; positions are net of trades. Search: documents contain terms; queries match terms.)
3. **Identify the dynamics.** How does the state change over time? (Chess: moves change piece positions. Trading: orders are added, modified, canceled, filled. Search: documents are indexed; queries are evaluated.)

The output of this process is a **conceptual model** of the problem — a mental picture of what the engine is reasoning about.

### Decoupling Physical Entity Representations from Working Runtime Data Structures

The conceptual model is *not* the same as the runtime data structure. The conceptual model is what the engine is reasoning about; the runtime data structure is how the engine represents it in memory.

For example, in chess:
- **Conceptual model:** 64 squares, each may contain a piece; one side moves at a time; certain special rules (castling, en passant).
- **Runtime data structure:** Eight 64-bit bitboards (one per piece type), plus 8 bytes of metadata.

The bitboards are a *compressed* representation of the conceptual model. The compression preserves all the information the engine needs (piece locations, side to move, special rules) while fitting in a small, cache-friendly footprint.

### The Compression Imperative

The runtime data structure should be **as small as possible** while preserving all the information the engine needs. Smaller is faster (fits in cache); smaller is cheaper (less memory); smaller is simpler (fewer things to update).

Compression techniques:

1. **Bit-packing.** Encode multiple values into a single machine word.
2. **Canonicalization.** Store only one representative of equivalent states.
3. **Differential encoding.** Store deltas from a reference state.
4. **Hashing.** Store hashes for comparison (not for reconstruction).
5. **Implicit representation.** Compute derived values on demand rather than storing them.

The goal: hot state fits in L1 cache (32 KB). Warm state fits in L2 (256 KB). Cold state can be in DRAM.

### The Information-Preservation Test

A compressed representation is correct if and only if it preserves all the information the engine needs to make its decisions. Test this by asking:

1. **Can the engine reconstruct the conceptual model from the runtime representation?** (Not necessarily literally, but functionally — can it answer any question about the conceptual model?)
2. **Are there two distinct conceptual states that map to the same runtime representation?** (If so, the compression is lossy in a way that may matter.)
3. **Are there runtime states that do not correspond to any conceptual state?** (If so, the representation has redundancies or invalid states.)

If the answer to (1) is "yes" and the answers to (2) and (3) are "no", the compression is correct.

---

## 6.1.2 Ensuring Memory Contiguity

The second task of Phase 1 is to ensure the state is laid out in memory **contiguously** — packed into a small number of cache lines, with no wasted space.

### The Cache-Line Budget

A cache line is 64 bytes. The hot state should fit in as few cache lines as possible:

| Cache lines | Bytes | Cache level | Access time |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 64 | L1 | 1 ns |
| 4 | 256 | L1 | 1 ns |
| 32 | 2 KB | L1 | 1 ns |
| 512 | 32 KB | L1 (max) | 1 ns |
| 4096 | 256 KB | L2 (max) | 4 ns |
| 500K | 30 MB | L3 (max) | 12 ns |
| ∞ | ∞ | DRAM | 100 ns |

Design target: hot state fits in 1–4 cache lines (64–256 bytes). This gives 1 ns access time.

### Designing for Contiguity

To make the state contiguous:

1. **Pack fields into a single struct.** Do not use pointer-based linked structures.
2. **Align the struct to a cache line boundary.** `alignas(64) struct HotState { ... };`.
3. **Separate hot and cold fields.** If the struct has hot fields (touched every iteration) and cold fields (touched rarely), split into two structs.
4. **Use fixed-size arrays, not dynamic containers.** `Order orders[MAX_ORDERS]` is contiguous; `std::vector<Order>` may not be (and has allocation overhead).
5. **Avoid padding.** Order fields from largest to smallest to minimize padding.

### Example: Cache-Line-Aligned Chess State

```c
struct alignas(64) ChessState {
    // 8 bitboards (8 bytes each) = 64 bytes
    uint64_t white_pawns;
    uint64_t white_knights;
    uint64_t white_bishops;
    uint64_t white_rooks;
    uint64_t white_queens;
    uint64_t white_king;
    uint64_t black_pawns;
    uint64_t black_knights;
    // ... 4 more bitboards for black, but we're out of space in this cache line
};

// Better: split into two cache lines
struct alignas(64) ChessState {
    uint64_t bitboards[12];   // 96 bytes — spans 2 cache lines
    uint8_t side_to_move;     // 1 byte
    uint8_t castling_rights;  // 1 byte (4 bits used)
    uint8_t en_passant;       // 1 byte (square index, 0 = none)
    uint8_t halfmove_clock;   // 1 byte
    uint16_t fullmove_number; // 2 bytes
    uint32_t padding;         // 4 bytes
    uint64_t zobrist_hash;    // 8 bytes
    // Total: 96 + 8 + 8 = 112 bytes — 2 cache lines
};
```

112 bytes fits in 2 cache lines — 2 ns to read the entire state from L1.

### The Hot-Cold Split

If a struct has both hot and cold fields, split them:

```c
// Hot: touched every iteration of the search
struct alignas(64) ChessStateHot {
    uint64_t bitboards[12];   // 96 bytes
    uint64_t zobrist_hash;    // 8 bytes
    // Plus 56 bytes of padding to fill the second cache line
};  // 128 bytes — 2 cache lines

// Cold: touched rarely (e.g., for display, logging)
struct ChessStateCold {
    uint8_t side_to_move;
    uint8_t castling_rights;
    uint8_t en_passant;
    uint8_t halfmove_clock;
    uint16_t fullmove_number;
    char fen_string[80];     // for display
    MoveList move_history;   // for threefold repetition
};
```

The hot struct is in L1 every iteration. The cold struct is fetched only when needed (e.g., for displaying the position).

---

## 6.1.3 The State Design Process

Designing the state is an iterative process. The steps:

### Step 1: List All Fields

Start by listing every field the engine might need:

- Position (board, order book, document, etc.)
- Side / role / actor
- History (for undo, repetition detection, audit)
- Counters (move number, iteration number)
- Time budget remaining
- Cache pointers (transposition table, etc.)
- Statistics (nodes searched, cache hits, etc.)

### Step 2: Classify Each Field

For each field, classify:
- **Hot or cold?** Is it touched every iteration, or rarely?
- **Mutable or immutable?** Does it change during execution?
- **Derivable or stored?** Can it be computed from other fields?
- **Bounded or unbounded?** Does it have a known maximum size?

### Step 3: Eliminate Redundant Fields

If a field can be derived from other fields, eliminate it (unless caching the derivation is a deliberate optimization).

### Step 4: Pack the Hot Fields

Pack the hot fields into a cache-line-aligned struct. Use bit-packing to minimize size.

### Step 5: Pack the Cold Fields

Pack the cold fields into a separate struct. Order fields by size (largest first) to minimize padding.

### Step 6: Choose the Right Data Structures

For collections (lists, maps, sets), choose the right data structure:
- **Sequential access?** Flat array.
- **Random access by key?** Hash table (small) or B-tree (large).
- **Range queries?** B-tree or sorted array.
- **Similarity queries?** ANN structure (HNSW, IVF).

### Step 7: Document the Layout

Write a comment explaining the layout — why each field is where it is, what the alignment is, what the cache behavior is. Future you (and your colleagues) will thank you.

### Step 8: Benchmark

Measure the cache behavior of the state:
- `perf stat` to measure cache hit rate.
- Verify that the hot state fits in L1.
- If the cache hit rate is below 90%, redesign.

---

## 6.1.4 Common Pitfalls

### Pitfall 1: Object-Oriented State

Engineers trained in OOP tend to model state as objects with methods. This leads to pointer-based structures, virtual function calls, and poor cache behavior. Prefer plain structs with free functions.

### Pitfall 2: Storing Derivable Data

If a field can be derived from others, do not store it (unless derivation is expensive). Storing derivable data wastes memory and creates consistency bugs.

### Pitfall 3: Mixing Hot and Cold Data

Putting hot and cold fields in the same struct pollutes the cache with cold data. Split them.

### Pitfall 4: Pointer Chasing

Linked structures (linked lists, trees with pointer children) cause cache misses. Use array-based representations.

### Pitfall 5: Wrong Alignment

A struct that is 65 bytes wastes an entire cache line. Pad to 64, 128, or 192 bytes.

### Pitfall 6: No Documentation

State layout decisions are often subtle. Without documentation, future changes will break the layout (and the performance).

### Pitfall 7: Premature Optimization

Do not optimize the state layout before you have a working (if slow) engine. Get the engine working first, then optimize the state based on profiling.

### Pitfall 8: Not Testing State Operations

State operations (make_move, undo_move, serialize, deserialize) must be tested exhaustively. A bug in any of these corrupts the entire engine.

---

## 6.1.5 Important Reminders

- **State is the most important design decision.** Get it right first.
- **Hot state fits in L1 (32 KB).** Cold state can be in DRAM.
- **Use bit-packing.** Pack small fields into bytes/words.
- **Separate hot and cold fields.** Do not let cold data pollute the cache.
- **Use contiguous arrays, not linked structures.** Always.
- **Align structs to cache line boundaries.** `alignas(64)`.
- **Document the layout.** Future you will thank you.
- **Test state operations exhaustively.** Bugs here are catastrophic.
- **Profile cache behavior.** `perf stat`. Aim for > 90% L1/L2 hit rate.
- **Get a working engine first, then optimize.** Premature optimization wastes time.

---

## 6.1.6 Summary

Phase 1 of the engine lifecycle is high-fidelity state mapping: defining the state $S$ and context $C$ that the engine will reason about. This is the most important phase, because a poorly chosen state representation caps the engine's performance no matter how clever the algorithm.

The state design process has eight steps: list all fields, classify each field, eliminate redundant fields, pack the hot fields, pack the cold fields, choose the right data structures, document the layout, and benchmark. The goal is a cache-line-aligned, contiguous, minimal representation that preserves all the information the engine needs.

Common pitfalls include object-oriented state (pointer-based, poor cache behavior), storing derivable data (wastes memory), mixing hot and cold data (cache pollution), pointer chasing (cache misses), wrong alignment (wasted cache lines), no documentation, premature optimization, and insufficient testing.

With a well-designed state, the engine is ready for Phase 2: building the transition function $F$.

---

**Previous chapter:** [[4. Iterative Refinement Architectures]]
**Next note:** [[2. Phase 2 Building the Transition Function]]

# 4. Phase 4 — Profiling-Driven Micro-Optimization

> "Phase 3 made the engine 100× faster by replacing brute force with structure. Phase 4 squeezes out the last 2–5× by optimizing the hot loop at the cycle level — SIMD, branchless code, cache-line alignment, prefetching. This is the phase where 1% improvements matter, because by now the engine is fast enough that 1% is significant."

This is the fourth note in the engine lifecycle. Phase 1 produced the state, Phase 2 produced a correct $F$, Phase 3 made $F$ structurally fast, and Phase 4 micro-optimizes the hot loop. Phase 5 (next note) will cover event loop orchestration.

---

## 6.4.1 Locating Bottlenecks with Profiling Tools

The cardinal rule of Phase 4: **never optimize without profiling.** Intuition about bottlenecks is wrong ~80% of the time. Profile first; optimize the bottleneck.

### Profiling Tools

**Linux `perf`.** The standard profiler on Linux. Samples the CPU at a configurable rate and attributes samples to functions.

```bash
# Profile a program
$ perf record ./engine

# Report the hot functions
$ perf report

# Get statistics (cache misses, branch misses, IPC)
$ perf stat ./engine
```

`perf stat` output:
```
  1000000000 instructions
   200000000 cache misses
    50000000 branch misses
       2.5 GHz CPU
       3.5 IPC
       5.2 seconds wall time
```

An IPC (instructions per cycle) of 3.5 is good (the CPU is mostly executing, not waiting). An IPC of 0.5 is bad (the CPU is mostly waiting for memory).

**Intel VTune.** A more detailed profiler (commercial). Provides cache analysis, memory access patterns, and hot spots.

**AMD μProf.** AMD's equivalent of VTune.

**macOS Instruments.** The "Counters" instrument provides cache stats on macOS.

**Valgrind Callgrind / Cachegrind.** Simulates the CPU; very slow but very accurate. Useful for cache analysis.

**gprof.** A compiler-assisted profiler. Adds instrumentation to the code; provides function call counts and times.

### What to Profile

Profile the engine under realistic load — the same input it will see in production. Profiling under artificial load gives misleading results.

Specifically, profile:
- **Total runtime.** The bottom-line metric.
- **Hot functions.** Which functions take the most time?
- **Cache behavior.** L1, L2, L3 hit rates; cache misses.
- **Branch behavior.** Branch misprediction rate.
- **Memory allocation.** Number of allocations, total bytes allocated.
- **I/O.** Bytes read/written, syscall count.

### Reading a Profile

A typical profile shows:

```
Samples: 100K
  35.2%  search.c:alpha_beta        # hot function 1
  18.7%  search.c:evaluate          # hot function 2
  10.3%  movegen.c:generate_moves   # hot function 3
   8.1%  hash.c:tt_lookup           # hot function 4
   5.4%  ...
   3.2%  ...
   ...    (long tail)
```

Focus on the top 3–5 functions. These account for ~75% of runtime. Optimizing them gives the biggest speedup. The long tail (functions with < 1% each) is not worth optimizing — even eliminating all of them gives < 25% speedup.

### Profiling Multi-Threaded Engines

For multi-threaded engines, profile each thread separately. Look for:
- **Load imbalance.** Some threads are idle while others are busy.
- **Lock contention.** Threads spend time waiting for locks.
- **Cache coherence traffic.** Threads invalidate each other's cache lines.

`perf` can profile multi-threaded engines; use `perf record -t <thread_id>` to profile a specific thread.

---

## 6.4.2 Eliminating Speculative Micro-Optimizations

Phase 4 is about **measured** optimization. "Speculative" optimizations — changes made based on intuition without measurement — are the enemy of Phase 4.

### The Speculative Optimization Trap

An engineer thinks: "Function X must be slow because it does Y. Let me optimize it by doing Z." They spend two days implementing Z. They measure. The engine is 0% faster — function X was not the bottleneck.

This is the speculative optimization trap. It wastes time and adds complexity without benefit.

### The Anti-Pattern: "Clever" Code

Speculative optimization often takes the form of "clever" code:
- Bit-twiddling hacks that are not measurably faster.
- Cache-line-aligned structs that are not in the hot path.
- SIMD intrinsics in functions that are not bottlenecks.
- Lock-free data structures where locks were not the bottleneck.

"Clever" code is harder to read, harder to maintain, and harder to debug. It should be added only when measured performance justifies it.

### The Disciplined Approach

The disciplined approach to Phase 4:

1. **Profile.** Identify the top 3–5 hot functions.
2. **Hypothesize.** Form a hypothesis about why each function is slow (cache misses? branch mispredictions? algorithm?).
3. **Test the hypothesis.** Use targeted profiling (cache stats, branch stats) to verify.
4. **Optimize.** Apply the optimization that addresses the verified bottleneck.
5. **Measure.** Verify the optimization improved performance.
6. **Verify correctness.** Run the test suite.
7. **Keep or revert.** If the optimization helped, keep it. If not, revert.

If the optimization did not help, **revert it.** Do not keep "clever" code that does not improve performance.

### Case Study: The Branchless Optimization That Did Not Help

An engineer notices a hot function has a branch: `if (x < threshold) y = a; else y = b;`. They replace it with branchless code: `y = (x < threshold) ? a : b;` (which compiles to a conditional move).

They measure. The function is 1% faster, but the engine is 0% faster — the function was not the bottleneck. The branchless code is harder to read. They revert.

Lesson: micro-optimizations only matter in the hot loop. Outside the hot loop, they are noise.

---

## 6.4.3 The Micro-Optimization Toolkit

When profiling identifies a bottleneck, apply the appropriate micro-optimization:

### For Memory Bottlenecks

If the hot function is memory-bound (low IPC, high cache miss rate):

- **Improve data layout.** AoS → SoA; pack hot fields; align to cache lines.
- **Prefetch.** Use `__builtin_prefetch` to load data before it is needed.
- **Reduce working set.** Eliminate unnecessary fields; use smaller types.
- **Use cache-friendly data structures.** Arrays over linked lists; flat hash tables over chained hash tables.

### For Branch Bottlenecks

If the hot function has high branch misprediction rate:

- **Make branches predictable.** Reorder code so the common case is the fall-through.
- **Eliminate branches.** Use conditional moves, lookup tables, branchless arithmetic.
- **Use SIMD.** SIMD comparisons are branchless.

### For Compute Bottlenecks

If the hot function is compute-bound (high IPC, low cache miss rate):

- **Use SIMD.** AVX2, AVX-512, NEON. Process 4–16 elements per instruction.
- **Reduce instruction count.** Replace complex operations with simpler ones (e.g., multiply by 2 → shift left by 1).
- **Inline the function.** Eliminate call overhead.
- **Use compiler optimizations.** `-O3`, `-march=native`, `-funroll-loops`.

### For Allocation Bottlenecks

If the hot function spends time in `malloc`/`free`:

- **Pre-allocate.** Allocate all memory at startup.
- **Use arena/pool allocators.** O(1) allocation; no per-allocation free.
- **Avoid `std::string` and `std::vector` in hot paths.** Use flat arrays with known sizes.
- **Use a fast allocator.** jemalloc, tcmalloc, mimalloc.

### For Lock Bottlenecks

If the hot function spends time waiting for locks:

- **Reduce critical sections.** Move work outside the lock.
- **Use sharded locks.** Partition the data; each shard has its own lock.
- **Use lock-free data structures.** Atomic operations, RCU, hazard pointers.
- **Use per-thread data.** Each thread has its own copy; no synchronization needed.

---

## 6.4.4 Verification and Benchmarking

Every micro-optimization must be verified to actually improve performance. The verification process:

### Step 1: Establish a Baseline

Before the optimization, measure the engine's performance under realistic load. Use a representative benchmark (e.g., a chess engine's benchmark suite, a search engine's query log).

Record:
- Total runtime.
- Hot function runtimes (from profiler).
- Cache hit rates.
- Branch misprediction rates.
- IPC.

### Step 2: Apply the Optimization

Make the change. Keep the diff small — one optimization at a time. If you make multiple changes, you cannot tell which one helped.

### Step 3: Re-Measure

Run the same benchmark. Record the same metrics.

### Step 4: Compare

Compare before and after:
- Did total runtime improve?
- Did the hot function's runtime improve?
- Did cache hit rates improve?
- Did branch misprediction rates improve?

### Step 5: Verify Correctness

Run the test suite. The engine must produce the same output (or statistically equivalent output, for non-deterministic engines).

### Step 6: Keep or Revert

If the optimization improved performance and did not break correctness, keep it. Otherwise, revert.

### Benchmarking Best Practices

- **Run multiple times.** Single runs are noisy. Run the benchmark 5–10 times and take the median.
- **Use a realistic benchmark.** A benchmark that does not represent production load gives misleading results.
- **Pin the CPU frequency.** Modern CPUs scale frequency dynamically; this adds noise. Pin the frequency (e.g., `cpufreq-set -f 3GHz`) for consistent measurements.
- **Isolate the machine.** Other processes on the machine add noise. Run benchmarks on a dedicated machine.
- **Measure with the right tool.** Use `perf stat` for cache/branch stats, `time` or `perf record` for runtime. Do not trust the engine's internal timers — they may not include I/O or kernel time.

---

## 6.4.5 Common Pitfalls

### Pitfall 1: Optimizing Without Profiling

The cardinal sin. Profile first; optimize the bottleneck.

### Pitfall 2: Optimizing the Wrong Function

The top function in the profile is the bottleneck. The 10th function is not. Focus on the top.

### Pitfall 3: Keeping "Clever" Code That Does Not Help

If an optimization does not measurably improve performance, revert it. "Clever" code that does not help is technical debt.

### Pitfall 4: Not Verifying Correctness

An optimization that breaks correctness is a regression, not an improvement. Run the test suite.

### Pitfall 5: Making Multiple Changes at Once

If you make multiple changes and the engine gets faster, you do not know which change helped. Make one change at a time.

### Pitfall 6: Noisy Measurements

Single runs are noisy. Run multiple times and take the median. Pin CPU frequency.

### Pitfall 7: Wrong Benchmark

A benchmark that does not represent production load gives misleading results. Use a realistic benchmark.

### Pitfall 8: Over-Optimizing

Phase 4 gives 2–5× speedup. After that, further optimization gives diminishing returns. Know when to stop.

### Pitfall 9: Not Considering Maintainability

Micro-optimized code is harder to read and maintain. Weigh the performance gain against the maintenance cost.

### Pitfall 10: Ignoring the Compiler

The compiler is good at micro-optimization. Let it do its job with `-O3` and `-march=native`. Manual micro-optimization is needed only for cases the compiler cannot handle.

---

## 6.4.6 Important Reminders

- **Never optimize without profiling.** Intuition is wrong 80% of the time.
- **Focus on the top 3–5 hot functions.** The long tail is not worth optimizing.
- **One change at a time.** Otherwise you cannot tell what helped.
- **Measure before and after.** Use `perf stat`, `perf record`, VTune.
- **Verify correctness.** Run the test suite.
- **Revert optimizations that do not help.** "Clever" code that does not measurably improve is technical debt.
- **Match the optimization to the bottleneck.** Memory, branch, compute, allocation, or lock.
- **Use the compiler.** `-O3`, `-march=native`, `-funroll-loops`. Let it do its job.
- **Run multiple times.** Take the median. Pin CPU frequency.
- **Use a realistic benchmark.** Production-like load gives meaningful results.
- **Know when to stop.** Phase 4 gives 2–5×. Beyond that, diminishing returns.

---

## 6.4.7 Summary

Phase 4 of the engine lifecycle is profiling-driven micro-optimization. The cardinal rule: never optimize without profiling. Use `perf`, VTune, or similar tools to identify the top 3–5 hot functions, then apply the appropriate micro-optimization (memory, branch, compute, allocation, or lock) based on the verified bottleneck.

The disciplined approach: profile, hypothesize, test the hypothesis, optimize, measure, verify correctness, keep or revert. Never keep "clever" code that does not measurably improve performance.

Common pitfalls include optimizing without profiling, optimizing the wrong function, keeping code that does not help, not verifying correctness, making multiple changes at once, noisy measurements, wrong benchmarks, over-optimizing, ignoring maintainability, and ignoring the compiler.

With Phase 4 complete, the engine is as fast as it can reasonably be. Phase 5 (next note) covers event loop orchestration — wrapping the engine in a runtime that handles external events.

---

**Previous note:** [[3. Phase 3 Structural Optimizations over Brute-Force]]
**Next note:** [[5. Phase 5 Event Loop Orchestration]]

# 2. Phase 2 — Building the Transition Function

> "Phase 1 gave you the state; Phase 2 gives you the engine's *behavior*. The transition function $F$ is where the engine's logic lives — where 'a chess position' becomes 'a chess move', where 'a search query' becomes 'a ranked result list', where 'a market event' becomes 'a trading decision'. Get $F$ right, and the engine does the right thing. Get it wrong, and no amount of optimization will fix the correctness."

This is the second note in the engine lifecycle. Phase 1 produced a well-designed state; Phase 2 builds the transition function $F$ that operates on that state.

---

## 6.2.1 Modular Functional Composition

The first principle of $F$ design is **modular composition**: $F$ should be a pipeline of small, independent, testable functions, not a monolithic block of code.

### The Three Stages of $F$

Recall from Chapter 1 that $F$ decomposes into three stages:

1. **Generate.** Produce the candidate set.
2. **Evaluate.** Score each candidate.
3. **Select.** Pick the best candidate and commit.

```python
def F(state, context):
    candidates = generate(state, context)
    scored = evaluate(candidates, state, context)
    next_state = select(scored, candidates, state, context)
    return next_state
```

Each stage is a separate function with a clear input and output. This separation enables:

- **Independent testing.** Each stage can be tested in isolation.
- **Independent optimization.** Each stage can be optimized without affecting the others.
- **Independent replacement.** A stage can be replaced with a different implementation (e.g., replacing a heuristic evaluator with a learned model).
- **Clear profiling.** Each stage's cost can be measured separately.

### Sub-Decomposition Within Stages

Each stage can itself be decomposed:

**Generate:**
```python
def generate(state, context):
    pseudo = generate_pseudo_candidates(state)
    legal = filter_illegal(pseudo, state)
    return legal
```

**Evaluate:**
```python
def evaluate(candidates, state, context):
    scores = []
    for c in candidates:
        score = sum(scorer.weight * scorer.fn(c, state, context) 
                    for scorer in context.scorers)
        scores.append(score)
    return scores
```

**Select:**
```python
def select(scored, candidates, state, context):
    best_idx = argmax(scored)
    return commit(candidates[best_idx], state, context)
```

Each sub-function is small, focused, and testable.

### The Pipeline Pattern

For more complex $F$ (e.g., search engine query evaluation), the pipeline may have many stages:

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    Q[Query] --> T[Tokenize]
    T --> A[Analyze: stem, stopwords]
    A --> R[Rewrite]
    R --> L[Lookup in index]
    L → M[Merge posting lists]
    M → S[Score with BM25]
    S → P[Personalize]
    P → F[Filter: safety, dedup]
    F → O[Output: top-k]
```

Each stage is a function: input → output. The stages are composed with simple function calls. This pattern is easy to read, easy to test, and easy to profile.

### When to Decompose

Decompose when:
- A function is more than ~50 lines of code.
- A function has multiple responsibilities.
- A function has multiple branches that are not mutually exclusive.
- A function is hard to test in isolation.

Do not decompose when:
- The function is already small and focused.
- Decomposition would create functions that are not reusable.
- The decomposition adds more overhead (function call cost) than it saves in clarity.

For hot loops, beware of over-decomposition: each function call has overhead. Inline the hottest functions manually or with compiler hints.

---

## 6.2.2 Guaranteeing Determinism

The second principle of $F$ design is **determinism**: given the same state and context, $F$ should produce the same output. Determinism is essential for:

1. **Testing.** You cannot test a non-deterministic function; the output changes between runs.
2. **Debugging.** A bug that appears only sometimes is nearly impossible to diagnose.
3. **Reproducibility.** If the engine's behavior cannot be reproduced, you cannot verify that a fix works.
4. **Concurrency.** Non-deterministic behavior in multi-threaded engines leads to race conditions.

### Sources of Non-Determinism

Common sources of non-determinism in $F$:

1. **Random number generation.** MCTS, sampling, randomized algorithms.
2. **Wall-clock time.** Time-based termination, time-based features.
3. **Hash table iteration order.** Hash tables do not preserve insertion order; iterating gives different orders across runs.
4. **Floating-point operations.** Floating-point arithmetic is not associative; `a + b + c` may differ from `a + (b + c)`.
5. **Multi-threading.** Without explicit synchronization, threads may execute in different orders.
6. **External state.** Reading from files, network, or environment variables.
7. **Memory allocation.** Allocators may return different addresses across runs; pointer comparison is non-deterministic.

### Achieving Determinism

To achieve determinism:

1. **Use seeded RNG.** Always seed the random number generator with a fixed seed. Store the seed in the engine's configuration.
2. **Encode time in state.** Instead of checking `now() > deadline`, check `state.iterations > max_iterations` or `state.deadline > state.current_time`. The "current time" is a field of state, set explicitly at startup.
3. **Use ordered collections.** Use sorted maps or vectors of pairs instead of hash tables when iteration order matters. Or sort the hash table's output before iterating.
4. **Use fixed-point or rational arithmetic.** For exact arithmetic, use integers (e.g., represent prices as integer ticks) or rational numbers.
5. **Synchronize explicitly.** Use locks, atomics, or single-threaded execution for the hot path.
6. **Avoid external state in the hot path.** Read all configuration at startup; do not read files or environment variables during execution.
7. **Avoid pointer comparison.** Compare by value, not by address.

### The Determinism Test

To verify determinism:

1. Run the engine twice with the same input and seed.
2. Compare the outputs byte-for-byte.
3. If they differ, the engine is non-deterministic. Find and fix the source.

For engines that use floating-point, this test may reveal subtle non-determinism from FP non-associativity. The fix is to use fixed-point or to accept the non-determinism (with documented error bounds).

### Determinism in Testing

For testing, determinism is mandatory. A test that fails sometimes is worse than no test. To make tests deterministic:

1. Use a fixed RNG seed in tests.
2. Mock the wall clock.
3. Use single-threaded execution.
4. Avoid floating-point in assertions (or use tolerance-based comparison).

### When Non-Determinism Is Acceptable

Non-determinism is acceptable when:
- The engine's output is judged statistically (e.g., win rate over many games), not per-decision.
- The non-determinism is bounded (e.g., MCTS gives different moves per run, but the move quality is similar).
- The user accepts approximate answers (e.g., search engines may return slightly different results per query due to A/B testing).

Even in these cases, the engine should be deterministic *given the same seed*. The seed is the lever for reproducibility.

---

## 6.2.3 The Build Process for $F$

Building $F$ is an iterative process:

### Step 1: Define the Interface

Start by defining the interface of $F$:

```python
def F(state: State, context: Context) -> State:
    """Apply one step of the engine to the state.
    
    Args:
        state: The current engine state.
        context: The engine context (caches, models, config).
    
    Returns:
        The next engine state.
    
    Raises:
        TerminalState: If the state is terminal (no further transitions).
    """
```

The interface should be clear, with documented inputs, outputs, and exceptions. This interface will not change much as the engine evolves.

### Step 2: Implement the Stages

Implement each stage (generate, evaluate, select) as a separate function. Initially, use the simplest correct implementation — do not optimize.

```python
def generate(state, context):
    # Simplest correct implementation
    return all_possible_actions(state)

def evaluate(candidates, state, context):
    # Simplest correct implementation
    return [simple_score(c, state) for c in candidates]

def select(scored, candidates, state, context):
    # Simplest correct implementation
    best_idx = max(range(len(scored)), key=lambda i: scored[i])
    return apply(candidates[best_idx], state)
```

The goal at this stage is **correctness**, not performance. A correct slow engine is a foundation for optimization; a fast incorrect engine is useless.

### Step 3: Test the Stages

Test each stage in isolation:

```python
def test_generate():
    state = create_test_state()
    candidates = generate(state, test_context())
    assert len(candidates) > 0
    assert all(is_valid(c, state) for c in candidates)

def test_evaluate():
    candidates = [test_candidate_1(), test_candidate_2()]
    scores = evaluate(candidates, test_state(), test_context())
    assert len(scores) == len(candidates)
    assert all(isinstance(s, (int, float)) for s in scores)

def test_select():
    candidates = [test_candidate_1(), test_candidate_2()]
    scores = [1.0, 2.0]
    next_state = select(scores, candidates, test_state(), test_context())
    assert next_state is not None
```

Each test should be small, focused, and deterministic.

### Step 4: Test $F$ End-to-End

Test $F$ as a whole:

```python
def test_F():
    state = create_test_state()
    next_state = F(state, test_context())
    assert next_state is not state  # F should produce a new state
    assert is_valid_state(next_state)
```

End-to-end tests verify that the stages compose correctly.

### Step 5: Test the Engine Loop

Test the full engine loop:

```python
def test_engine_loop():
    state = create_initial_state()
    context = create_test_context()
    while not is_terminal(state):
        state = F(state, context)
    assert is_valid_terminal_state(state)
```

This test runs the engine to completion and verifies the terminal state.

### Step 6: Add Optimizations (Phase 3)

Once the engine is correct, add optimizations (covered in the next note). Each optimization is tested against the unoptimized version to verify it does not break correctness.

### Step 7: Profile (Phase 4)

Profile the engine to identify bottlenecks (covered in the note after next). Optimize the bottlenecks, not the parts that are already fast.

---

## 6.2.4 The Discipline of Side Effects

$F$ is conceptually pure: it takes state and context, returns a new state. In practice, $F$ may have side effects: updating caches, modifying context, logging.

The discipline of side effects:

1. **Side effects must not affect the current call's output.** Two calls to $F$ with the same state and context must return the same next state, regardless of side effects.
2. **Side effects must be deferred until after the result is computed.** Compute the next state, then update caches and context.
3. **Side effects must be thread-safe.** Use atomics, locks, or per-thread buffers.

```python
def F(state, context):
    # Compute the next state (pure)
    candidates = generate(state, context)
    scored = evaluate(candidates, state, context)
    next_state = select(scored, candidates, state, context)
    
    # Side effects (after the result is computed)
    context.cache[state.hash] = next_state
    context.stats.nodes_processed += 1
    
    return next_state
```

This discipline ensures that $F$ remains deterministic (modulo side effects) and testable.

---

## 6.2.5 Common Pitfalls

### Pitfall 1: Monolithic $F$

A single function that does everything is unmaintainable, untestable, and unoptimizable. Decompose into stages.

### Pitfall 2: Premature Optimization

Optimizing $F$ before it is correct wastes time. Get a correct slow engine first, then optimize based on profiling.

### Pitfall 3: Non-Deterministic Behavior

Non-deterministic behavior makes the engine impossible to test and debug. Use seeded RNG, encoded time, ordered collections.

### Pitfall 4: Side Effects That Affect the Current Call

If a side effect changes the current call's output, the engine becomes non-deterministic. Defer side effects until after the result is computed.

### Pitfall 5: Untested Stages

Each stage should be tested in isolation. Without isolated tests, bugs in one stage are masked by other stages.

### Pitfall 6: Untested Engine Loop

The engine loop must be tested end-to-end. Without end-to-end tests, integration bugs go undetected.

### Pitfall 7: Wrong Abstraction Level

Decomposing too finely creates excessive function call overhead; decomposing too coarsely creates untestable monoliths. Find the right balance.

### Pitfall 8: Inlined Hot Functions

Inlined hot functions are fast but hard to test. Inline only after profiling identifies the function as a bottleneck.

### Pitfall 9: Forgetting to Handle Edge Cases

Edge cases (no candidates, all candidates tied, terminal state, etc.) must be handled explicitly. Unhandled edge cases cause crashes or wrong behavior.

### Pitfall 10: Not Documenting $F$

$F$'s interface, stages, and side effects should be documented. Without documentation, future changes will break the design.

---

## 6.2.6 Important Reminders

- **Decompose $F$ into generate, evaluate, select.** Each stage is independent and testable.
- **Use the simplest correct implementation first.** Optimize later.
- **Test each stage in isolation.** Then test $F$ end-to-end. Then test the engine loop.
- **Guarantee determinism.** Seeded RNG, encoded time, ordered collections.
- **Defer side effects until after the result is computed.** They affect future calls, not the current one.
- **Side effects must be thread-safe.** Atomics, locks, or per-thread buffers.
- **Do not optimize before profiling.** Get a correct slow engine first.
- **Document $F$'s interface, stages, and side effects.**

---

## 6.2.7 Summary

Phase 2 of the engine lifecycle is building the transition function $F$. The principles are:

1. **Modular functional composition.** $F$ decomposes into generate, evaluate, select. Each stage is a small, focused, testable function.
2. **Guaranteeing determinism.** $F$ produces the same output for the same input. Use seeded RNG, encoded time, ordered collections, and thread-safe side effects.
3. **The build process.** Define the interface, implement the stages (simply and correctly), test the stages, test $F$ end-to-end, test the engine loop, add optimizations, profile.
4. **The discipline of side effects.** Side effects must not affect the current call's output; they must be deferred until after the result is computed; they must be thread-safe.

Common pitfalls include monolithic $F$, premature optimization, non-deterministic behavior, side effects that affect the current call, untested stages, wrong abstraction level, inlined hot functions, unhandled edge cases, and missing documentation.

With a well-designed $F$, the engine is correct (if slow). Phase 3 (next note) covers structural optimizations to make it fast.

---

**Previous note:** [[1. Phase 1 High-Fidelity State Mapping]]
**Next note:** [[3. Phase 3 Structural Optimizations over Brute-Force]]

   Language: markdown

---
chapter: 1
section: 1.3
title: Core Mindset Shifts for Engine Engineers
tags:
