# 6. Real-World Reference Architectures to Study

> "The best way to learn engine architecture is to study real engines. Not toy examples, not simplified models — the actual codebases that power production systems. Lucene, RocksDB, TensorRT, XGBoost, Bullet Physics: these are the masterworks of engine engineering, and their source code is available for you to read."

This is the sixth and final note in Chapter 7. The previous notes covered the tools and libraries; this note covers the real-world engines that exemplify the principles of this course. Studying these engines is the capstone of engine engineering education.

---

## 7.6.1 Lucene / Elasticsearch Core

**Lucene** is the leading open-source text search engine library. **Elasticsearch** is a distributed search server built on Lucene. Together, they power Wikipedia, GitHub, Netflix, and thousands of other services.

### What to Study

1. **Inverted index structure.** How Lucene represents the mapping from terms to documents. Look at:
   - `SegmentInfo` — the metadata for an index segment.
   - `TermsEnum` — iteration over terms in an index.
   - `PostingsEnum` — iteration over postings (documents containing a term).
   - The compression codecs (LZ4, FST — Finite State Transducers).

2. **Query evaluation.** How Lucene evaluates a query:
   - `IndexSearcher.search()` — the entry point.
   - `Weight.scorer()` — creates a scorer for each query clause.
   - `Scorer.iterator()` — iterates over matching documents.
   - The `BulkScorer` API for batch scoring.

3. **Analyzer pipeline.** How text is tokenized, normalized, and filtered:
   - `Tokenizer` — splits text into tokens.
   - `TokenFilter` — applies transformations (lowercasing, stemming, stopword removal).
   - `Analyzer` — combines tokenizer and filters.

4. **Lucene's codec architecture.** How Lucene abstracts the on-disk format:
   - `Codec` — the top-level format abstraction.
   - `PostingsFormat` — how postings are stored.
   - `DocValuesFormat` — how per-document values are stored.

5. **Segment merging.** How Lucene merges index segments in the background:
   - `LogMergePolicy` — when to merge.
   - `TieredMergePolicy` — better merge selection.
   - The merge thread pool.

### Why Study Lucene

Lucene is the canonical example of:
- **Inverted index design.** The most studied inverted index implementation.
- **Query evaluation optimization.** WAND, MaxScore, block-based scoring.
- **Compression codecs.** FST, LZ4, Roaring Bitmaps.
- **Concurrent indexing and searching.** Near-real-time search with segment immutability.

**Where to start:** the Lucene source code is on GitHub (apache/lucene). Start with `IndexSearcher.java`, then dive into `TermScorer.java` and `FuzzyQuery.java`.

---

## 7.6.2 LevelDB / RocksDB

**LevelDB** is Google's embedded key-value store, designed by Jeff Dean and Sanjay Ghemawat. **RocksDB** is Facebook's fork of LevelDB, optimized for flash storage and high-throughput workloads.

### What to Study

1. **LSM tree structure.** How RocksDB organizes data:
   - **Memtable.** In-memory sorted structure (skiplist).
   - **SSTables.** Immutable on-disk sorted files.
   - **Write-ahead log (WAL).** For durability.
   - **Levels.** SSTables are organized into levels (L0, L1, ..., Lmax).

2. **Compaction.** How RocksDB merges SSTables:
   - **Level compaction.** Merge SSTables from level N to N+1.
   - **Universal compaction.** Alternative strategy optimized for write amplification.
   - **FIFO compaction.** For time-series data.

3. **Bloom filters.** How RocksDB avoids disk reads for non-existent keys:
   - Per-SSTable Bloom filters.
   - Prefix Bloom filters for range queries.

4. **Block cache.** How RocksDB caches frequently-accessed data blocks in memory.

5. **Write batch.** How RocksDB supports atomic multi-key writes.

### Why Study LevelDB / RocksDB

LevelDB and RocksDB are the canonical examples of:
- **LSM tree design.** The write-optimized counterpart to B-trees.
- **Write-ahead logging.** How to achieve durability without sacrificing performance.
- **Bloom filter usage.** How to use Bloom filters to avoid expensive disk reads.
- **Background compaction.** How to do maintenance work without blocking the foreground.
- **Cache design.** How to cache hot data while keeping cold data on disk.

**Where to start:** the LevelDB source code is small (~10,000 lines) and well-commented. Start with `db/db_impl.cc`, then look at `table/table.cc` and `util/bloom.cc`. RocksDB adds significant complexity; study LevelDB first.

---

## 7.6.3 TensorRT

**TensorRT** is NVIDIA's high-performance deep learning inference engine. It takes a trained neural network and optimizes it for NVIDIA GPUs, achieving 2–10× speedup over frameworks like PyTorch.

### What to Study

1. **Graph optimization.** How TensorRT optimizes the neural network graph:
   - **Layer fusion.** Combine consecutive layers (e.g., Conv + ReLU + Bias) into a single kernel.
   - **Precision calibration.** Convert FP32 weights to FP16 or INT8 with minimal accuracy loss.
   - **Kernel auto-tuning.** Select the best GPU kernel for each layer from a library of implementations.

2. **Memory management.** How TensorRT minimizes GPU memory usage:
   - **Memory pooling.** Reuse memory across layers.
   - **Static memory planning.** Allocate all memory upfront; no allocation during inference.

3. **Kernel fusion.** How TensorRT combines operations to reduce kernel launch overhead:
   - Element-wise operations (add, multiply, ReLU) are fused into a single kernel.
   - Convolutions are fused with following activations and normalizations.

4. **INT8 quantization.** How TensorRT converts FP32 models to INT8:
   - Calibration: run the model on representative input; collect activation statistics.
   - Quantization: convert weights and activations to INT8 using the collected statistics.
   - Dequantization: convert INT8 outputs back to FP32 for the next layer.

### Why Study TensorRT

TensorRT is the canonical example of:
- **Inference engine design.** How to take a trained model and run it fast.
- **Graph optimization.** How to transform a computation graph for efficiency.
- **Kernel fusion.** How to combine operations to reduce overhead.
- **Quantization.** How to trade precision for performance.

**Where to start:** TensorRT is closed-source, but the documentation is extensive. Read the Developer Guide, then look at the samples in the `samples` directory of the TensorRT distribution.

For an open-source alternative, study **ONNX Runtime** (Microsoft) or **TVM** (Apache). Both provide similar functionality with open-source code.

---

## 7.6.4 XGBoost

**XGBoost** is the leading gradient-boosted decision tree library. It is used for tabular data classification and regression in finance, advertising, and many other domains.

### What to Study

1. **Tree construction.** How XGBoost builds decision trees:
   - **Histogram-based splits.** Pre-compute histograms of feature values; use them to find the best split.
   - **Sparsity-aware split finding.** Handle missing values efficiently.
   - **Approximate split finding.** Use quantiles for approximate splits on large datasets.

2. **Cache-aware access.** How XGBoost organizes data for cache-friendly access:
   - **Column blocks.** Store data in column-major order (one column at a time).
   - **Compressed columns.** Compress sparse columns.

3. **Out-of-core computation.** How XGBoost handles datasets larger than RAM:
   - **Block compression.** Compress blocks on disk.
   - **Sharding.** Distribute blocks across multiple disks.

4. **Distributed training.** How XGBoost scales to multiple machines:
   - **Data-parallel training.** Each worker has a subset of the data; gradients are aggregated.
   - **Feature-parallel training.** Each worker has a subset of the features; splits are aggregated.

### Why Study XGBoost

XGBoost is the canonical example of:
- **Cache-aware algorithm design.** How to lay out data for cache-friendly access.
- **Sparsity-aware computation.** How to handle missing values efficiently.
- **Out-of-core computation.** How to handle datasets larger than RAM.
- **Distributed training.** How to scale ML training to multiple machines.

**Where to start:** the XGBoost source code is on GitHub (dmlc/xgboost). Start with `src/tree/updater_histmaker.cc` (histogram-based splits), then look at `src/common/column_matrix.h` (cache-aware data layout).

---

## 7.6.5 Bullet Physics Engine

**Bullet Physics** is an open-source physics simulation engine, used in games (Grand Theft Auto, Red Dead Redemption), movies (Hugo, Sherlock Holmes), and robotics research.

### What to Study

1. **Broadphase collision detection.** How Bullet quickly eliminates pairs of objects that cannot be colliding:
   - **Sweep and prune.** Sort objects by their bounding box minima; only check pairs whose bounding boxes overlap.
   - **Dynamic AABB tree.** A bounding volume hierarchy that is updated as objects move.

2. **Narrowphase collision detection.** How Bullet computes the exact contact points for pairs of objects that may be colliding:
   - **GJK (Gilbert-Johnson-Keerthi) algorithm.** Computes the closest distance between two convex shapes.
   - **EPA (Expanding Polytope Algorithm).** Computes the penetration depth when shapes overlap.
   - **SAT (Separating Axis Theorem).** For convex polyhedra.

3. **Constraint solving.** How Bullet resolves collisions and other constraints:
   - **Sequential impulse solver.** Iteratively applies impulses to satisfy constraints.
   - **Projected Gauss-Seidel solver.** Alternative solver for more complex constraint systems.

4. **Continuous collision detection.** How Bullet handles fast-moving objects:
   - **Conservative advancement.** Iteratively advances the objects; checks for collision at each step.
   - **Time of impact.** Computes the exact time at which two objects will collide.

### Why Study Bullet Physics

Bullet Physics is the canonical example of:
- **Broadphase / narrowphase design.** The two-phase collision detection pattern used in all physics engines.
- **Constraint solving.** How to resolve multiple interacting constraints iteratively.
- **Continuous collision detection.** How to handle fast-moving objects without missing collisions.
- **Real-time simulation loops.** How to run a complex simulation within a fixed time budget.

**Where to start:** the Bullet source code is on GitHub (bulletphysics/bullet3). Start with `src/BulletCollision/BroadphaseCollision/btDbvt.cpp` (dynamic AABB tree), then look at `src/BulletDynamics/ConstraintSolver/btSequentialImpulseConstraintSolver.cpp` (constraint solving).

---

## 7.6.6 Other Engines Worth Studying

Beyond the five above, these engines are also worth studying:

### Stockfish (Chess Engine)

The strongest open-source chess engine. Exemplifies:
- **Bitboard-based state representation.**
- **Alpha-beta search with all modern pruning techniques.**
- **NNUE (efficiently updatable neural network) evaluation.**
- **Iterative deepening with any-time behavior.**
- **Parallel search with lazy SMP.**

**Where to start:** the Stockfish source code is on GitHub (official-stockfish/Stockfish). Start with `src/search.cpp`, then look at `src/evaluate.cpp` (NNUE) and `src/movegen.cpp` (bitboard move generation).

### Redis (In-Memory Database)

A high-performance in-memory key-value store. Exemplifies:
- **Single-threaded event loop.** All operations are O(1) or O(log N); no locks needed.
- **Efficient data structures.** Sorted sets (skiplist), hashes (ziplist for small, hash table for large), lists (quicklist).
- **Persistence.** RDB (snapshot) and AOF (append-only log).
- **Pipelining.** Batch multiple commands in a single network round-trip.

**Where to start:** the Redis source code is on GitHub (redis/redis). Start with `src/server.c` (event loop), then look at `src/t_string.c` (string commands) and `src/t_zset.c` (sorted set).

### PostgreSQL (Relational Database)

The leading open-source relational database. Exemplifies:
- **MVCC (Multi-Version Concurrency Control).** Readers do not block writers; writers do not block readers.
- **WAL (Write-Ahead Log).** For durability and replication.
- **Query planner.** Cost-based optimization of SQL queries.
- **B-tree index.** The primary index structure.
- **VACUUM.** Background cleanup of dead tuples.

**Where to start:** the PostgreSQL source code is on GitHub (postgres/postgres). Start with `src/backend/tcop/postgres.c` (top-level command processing), then look at `src/backend/optimizer/plan/planer.c` (query planner) and `src/backend/access/nbtree/nbtpage.c` (B-tree).

### Nginx (Web Server)

A high-performance web server and reverse proxy. Exemplifies:
- **Event-driven architecture.** Single-threaded per worker; handles thousands of connections.
- **Master-worker model.** Master process manages worker processes; workers handle connections.
- **HTTP pipeline.** Modular processing of HTTP requests.
- **Reverse proxy with caching.** Cache responses from upstream servers.

**Where to start:** the Nginx source code is at nginx.org. Start with `src/core/nginx.c` (main loop), then look at `src/http/ngx_http_request.c` (HTTP request handling).

### V8 (JavaScript Engine)

Google's JavaScript engine, used in Chrome and Node.js. Exemplifies:
- **JIT compilation.** Just-in-time compilation of JavaScript to machine code.
- **Garbage collection.** Generational GC with concurrent marking and sweeping.
- **Hidden classes.** Optimization for dynamic object shapes.
- **Inline caching.** Cache the result of method lookups.

**Where to start:** the V8 source code is on GitHub (v8/v8). Start with `src/d8/d8.cc` (the shell), then look at `src/objects/objects.h` (object model) and `src/compiler/` (JIT compiler).

---

## 7.6.7 How to Study an Engine

Studying an engine is a skill. The process:

### Step 1: Read the Documentation

Read the official documentation first. Understand what the engine does, what its API is, and how it is used. Do not dive into the source code until you understand the user-facing behavior.

### Step 2: Read the High-Level Architecture

Find a high-level architecture document (or a technical paper). Understand the major components and how they interact.

### Step 3: Read the Entry Points

Find the entry points — the functions that are called first. For a search engine, this is the query evaluation function. For a database, this is the command processing function. Read these functions to understand the high-level flow.

### Step 4: Trace a Single Request

Trace a single request through the engine. Use a debugger, add print statements, or read the code carefully. Understand each step the request goes through.

### Step 5: Dive into One Component

Pick one component that interests you (e.g., the inverted index, the B-tree, the query planner). Read its source code in depth. Understand its data structures, algorithms, and optimizations.

### Step 6: Modify and Experiment

Make a small modification (e.g., add a new metric, change a parameter, add a new feature). Build the engine with your modification. Run the tests. This is the deepest form of learning — you understand the code by changing it.

### Step 7: Write Up Your Findings

Write a blog post or a document explaining what you learned. Teaching is the best way to learn.

---

## 7.6.8 Common Pitfalls

### Pitfall 1: Trying to Understand Everything at Once

Engines are large. You cannot understand everything in one sitting. Focus on one component at a time.

### Pitfall 2: Skipping the Documentation

Diving into source code without understanding the user-facing behavior leads to confusion. Read the docs first.

### Pitfall 3: Reading Code Passively

Reading code without modifying it is like reading a textbook without doing the exercises. You will not retain the knowledge. Modify, experiment, build.

### Pitfall 4: Studying Only One Engine

Each engine has its own design philosophy. Studying multiple engines gives you a broader perspective and helps you recognize patterns.

### Pitfall 5: Not Studying the Tests

The test suite is documentation of how the engine is supposed to behave. Read the tests to understand the engine's contract.

### Pitfall 6: Copying Code Without Understanding

Do not copy code from these engines into your own without understanding it. You will inherit its bugs without understanding its design.

### Pitfall 7: Not Reading the Git History

The git history shows why the code is the way it is. Look at the commit messages and the issue tracker to understand the design decisions.

### Pitfall 8: Giving Up Too Early

Engines are complex. The first read-through will be confusing. Persist; the second read-through is much clearer.

---

## 7.6.9 Important Reminders

- **Study real engines, not toy examples.** The principles are best learned from production code.
- **Lucene / Elasticsearch** for inverted indexes and query evaluation.
- **LevelDB / RocksDB** for LSM trees, write-ahead logging, and Bloom filters.
- **TensorRT** for inference engines, graph optimization, and quantization.
- **XGBoost** for cache-aware algorithm design and sparsity-aware computation.
- **Bullet Physics** for broadphase / narrowphase design and constraint solving.
- **Stockfish** for bitboards, alpha-beta, and NNUE.
- **Redis** for single-threaded event loops and efficient data structures.
- **PostgreSQL** for MVCC, WAL, and query planning.
- **Nginx** for event-driven architecture and HTTP pipelining.
- **V8** for JIT compilation and garbage collection.
- **Read the documentation, then the entry points, then trace a request, then dive into one component, then modify and experiment.**
- **Engines are large; study one component at a time.**
- **Reading passively is not enough; modify and experiment.**

---

## 7.6.10 Summary

The best way to learn engine architecture is to study real engines. Lucene, LevelDB/RocksDB, TensorRT, XGBoost, and Bullet Physics are the canonical examples of their respective domains. Each exemplifies specific design patterns and optimization techniques.

Beyond these five, study Stockfish (chess), Redis (in-memory database), PostgreSQL (relational database), Nginx (web server), and V8 (JavaScript engine). Each is a masterwork of engine engineering.

The process: read the documentation, then the high-level architecture, then the entry points, then trace a single request, then dive into one component, then modify and experiment, then write up your findings.

Common pitfalls include trying to understand everything at once, skipping the documentation, reading passively, studying only one engine, not studying the tests, copying code without understanding, not reading the git history, and giving up too early.

Studying real engines is the capstone of engine engineering education. With this course's principles and these reference architectures, you have everything you need to build your own fast, correct, and scalable engines.

---

This is the final note in the Engine Architecture Course. To review the entire course, start with [[1. Defining the Engine Concept in Modern Computation]].

chapter: 7

---

# 1. Low-Level System Languages

> "Engines are not written in Python. They are not written in JavaScript. They are not written in Java (with rare exceptions). They are written in C, C++, or Rust — the languages that give the engineer control over memory layout, instruction selection, and hardware interaction. The choice of language is the choice of how close to the metal you can get."

This is the first note in Chapter 7 — the system engineer's tooling and library blueprint. We start with the foundation: the programming language. Everything else (libraries, frameworks, tools) is built on this choice.

---

## 7.1.1 C / C++

C and C++ are the **base layer** of almost every real engine. Trading engines, databases, search engines, compilers, game engines — the vast majority are written in C or C++.

### Why C/C++?

1. **Deterministic memory control.** The engineer decides when memory is allocated and freed. No garbage collector, no pauses, no surprises. This is essential for latency-sensitive engines.

2. **Manual cache optimization.** The engineer controls the layout of structs in memory — alignment, padding, packing. The compiler does what the engineer tells it to, not what it thinks is best.

3. **Inline assembly access.** For the hottest loops, the engineer can drop down to assembly language. This is rarely needed (compilers are good), but available when necessary.

4. **Direct compiler instruction tuning.** Compiler flags like `-march=native`, `-mavx2`, `-mavx-512ev` allow the engineer to target specific CPU features.

5. **Zero-cost abstractions.** C++ templates, inline functions, and constexpr provide abstraction without runtime cost. `std::sort<int>` is as fast as a hand-written int sort.

6. **Mature ecosystem.** 50+ years of libraries, tools, and best practices. Every hardware platform has a C/C++ compiler.

### Where C/C++ Is Used

- **Trading engines.** Latency-critical; C++ is the default. Examples: Jump Trading, Citadel Securities, Jane Street (uses OCaml for some components, but C++ for the hot path).
- **Databases.** PostgreSQL (C), MySQL (C/C++), MongoDB (C++), Redis (C), SQLite (C).
- **Search engines.** Elasticsearch (Java, but with native C++ components via JNI), Lucene (Java).
- **Compilers.** GCC (C/C++), Clang (C++), V8 (C++), HotSpot (C++).
- **Game engines.** Unreal (C++), Unity (C++ core, C# scripting), idTech (C++).
- **Chess engines.** Stockfish (C++), Komodo (C++), Houdini (C++).

### The C++ Trade-off

C++ gives the engineer maximum control — and maximum responsibility. The engineer must:
- Manage memory manually (or use smart pointers correctly).
- Avoid undefined behavior (signed overflow, use-after-free, data races).
- Handle errors explicitly (no exceptions in hot paths).
- Compile for each target platform (no "write once, run anywhere").

For engines, this trade-off is favorable. The control is worth the responsibility. But for less performance-critical applications, the trade-off is unfavorable — a managed language (Go, Java, Python) is more productive.

### Modern C++ (C++17, C++20, C++23)

Modern C++ has evolved significantly from C++98. Features that improve engine code:

- **`constexpr` and `consteval`.** Compute at compile time. Move constant expressions out of runtime.
- **`std::array` and `std::string_view`.** Fixed-size arrays and non-owning string references. No allocation overhead.
- **`std::optional` and `std::variant`.** Type-safe alternatives to pointers and unions.
- **Move semantics.** Avoid copies of large objects.
- **RAII.** Resource Acquisition Is Initialization. Deterministic destruction; no manual cleanup.
- **Templates and concepts.** Generic programming without runtime cost.
- **Coroutines (C++20).** Async programming without callbacks.

### C vs C++

C is simpler, smaller, and easier to reason about. C++ is more expressive, more powerful, and more complex.

For new engine projects, **C++ is usually the better choice.** The expressiveness (templates, RAII, exceptions) outweighs the complexity, and modern C++ is much safer than C++98.

For projects that must interoperate with C-only libraries or must run on platforms without a C++ compiler, C is appropriate.

---

## 7.1.2 Rust

Rust is the **modern alternative** to C++. It provides C++-level performance with strong memory safety guarantees, enforced at compile time.

### Why Rust?

1. **Zero-cost abstractions.** Like C++, Rust's abstractions (iterators, traits, generics) compile to the same machine code as hand-written code.

2. **Memory safety without GC.** Rust's ownership system enforces memory safety at compile time. No use-after-free, no double-free, no data races — without a garbage collector. The performance cost is zero (or near-zero).

3. **Strong concurrency model.** Rust's `Send` and `Sync` traits make data races a compile-time error. Multi-threaded Rust code is provably safe.

4. **Modern tooling.** `cargo` (build tool and package manager), `rustfmt` (formatter), `clippy` (linter), `rustdoc` (documentation generator). The tooling is more consistent and modern than C++'s.

5. **No undefined behavior (in safe Rust).** Safe Rust cannot exhibit undefined behavior. Unsafe Rust can (for cases where the engineer needs raw pointer access), but unsafe code is explicitly marked and auditable.

### Where Rust Is Used

- **High-performance backend systems.** Cloudflare's pingora, Discord's read states service.
- **Distributed systems.** TiKV (distributed key-value store), InfluxDB IOx (time-series database).
- **Web browsers.** Parts of Firefox's rendering engine (Servo).
- **Operating systems.** Redox OS (a Rust-based OS); Linux kernel modules (experimental).
- **Game engines.** Bevy (an ECS-based game engine); Fyrox.
- **Trading.** Some HFT firms are experimenting with Rust, though C++ remains dominant.

### The Rust Trade-off

Rust's ownership system is a **learning curve.** Engineers must understand ownership, borrowing, and lifetimes. Code that would be trivial in C++ (e.g., a doubly-linked list) requires careful design in Rust.

For engines, this trade-off is favorable. The safety guarantees prevent entire classes of bugs (use-after-free, data races) that are catastrophic in production. The learning curve is a one-time cost; the safety is a permanent benefit.

### Safe vs Unsafe Rust

Rust distinguishes **safe** and **unsafe** code:

- **Safe Rust.** The compiler guarantees memory safety and thread safety. The vast majority of engine code should be safe.
- **Unsafe Rust.** The engineer takes responsibility for safety. Used for:
  - Calling C/C++ libraries (FFI).
  - Implementing low-level data structures (linked lists, ring buffers).
  - Hardware-specific intrinsics (SIMD, atomics).
  - Performance-critical code where the safe abstraction is too slow.

Unsafe code is marked with the `unsafe` keyword, making it easy to audit. A well-engineered Rust codebase has < 5% unsafe code, confined to specific modules.

### Rust for Engines — Recommended Libraries

- **`tokio`** — async runtime (event loop, tasks, I/O).
- **`rayon`** — data parallelism (parallel iterators).
- **`crossbeam`** — lock-free data structures (queues, skip lists).
- **`parking_lot`** — fast synchronization primitives (mutexes, RwLocks).
- **`bytes`** — zero-copy byte buffers.
- **`serde`** — serialization/deserialization.
- **` ndarray`** — N-dimensional arrays (for linear algebra).
- **`simde`** or `std::simd` — portable SIMD.

---

## 7.1.3 Choosing Between C++ and Rust

| Criterion | C++ | Rust |
|---|---|---|
| Performance | Equal | Equal |
| Memory safety | Manual | Compile-time guaranteed |
| Concurrency safety | Manual | Compile-time guaranteed |
| Learning curve | Steep (but familiar) | Steeper (ownership) |
| Ecosystem maturity | Very mature | Maturing rapidly |
| Talent pool | Large | Smaller, growing |
| Tooling | Fragmented | Unified (cargo) |
| Interop with C | Native | Via FFI |
| Interop with other C++ | Native | Limited |

**Choose C++ when:**
- The team has deep C++ expertise.
- The project must interoperate with existing C++ code.
- The project needs libraries that are only available in C++.
- The project targets platforms without a mature Rust compiler.

**Choose Rust when:**
- The team is willing to learn Rust.
- Memory safety is critical (e.g., security-sensitive engines).
- Concurrency safety is critical.
- The project is new (no existing C++ to interop with).
- Modern tooling is important.

For **greenfield** engine projects today, Rust is increasingly the right choice. For **existing** C++ codebases, migrating to Rust is expensive and rarely worth it — improve the C++ instead.

---

## 7.1.4 Languages to Avoid for Engine Cores

Some languages are unsuitable for engine cores, despite being excellent for other purposes:

### Python

- **Pros:** Excellent for prototyping, scripting, data analysis, ML training.
- **Cons:** Interpreted; GIL prevents true multi-threading; dynamic typing prevents optimization.
- **Verdict:** Use for prototyping and tooling, not for the engine core. If the prototype is too slow, rewrite in C/C++/Rust.

### JavaScript / Node.js

- **Pros:** Excellent for I/O-bound web servers; large ecosystem.
- **Cons:** Interpreted (V8 JIT helps but is not enough for engine cores); garbage collection pauses.
- **Verdict:** Use for the web tier, not for the engine core.

### Java

- **Pros:** Mature ecosystem; JIT compilation can be fast; excellent tooling.
- **Cons:** Garbage collection pauses (even with ZGC); object layout is not engineer-controlled (header overhead, alignment).
- **Verdict:** Suitable for engines where occasional GC pauses are acceptable (e.g., Elasticsearch, Cassandra). Not suitable for HFT or other latency-critical engines.

### Go

- **Pros:** Simple; fast compilation; excellent concurrency primitives (goroutines).
- **Cons:** Garbage collection; less control over memory layout than C++/Rust.
- **Verdict:** Suitable for high-throughput servers (gRPC services, API gateways). Not suitable for engine cores where latency or memory layout matters.

### C#

- **Pros:** Mature ecosystem; JIT compilation; excellent tooling (.NET).
- **Cons:** Garbage collection; less control over memory layout.
- **Verdict:** Suitable for game engines (Unity) and Windows-only engines. Not suitable for cross-platform latency-critical engines.

### Swift

- **Pros:** Modern; safe; fast.
- **Cons:** Apple-centric; smaller ecosystem outside Apple platforms.
- **Verdict:** Suitable for Apple-platform engines. Less suitable for cross-platform server engines.

---

## 7.1.5 The Hybrid Approach

Many engines use a **hybrid approach**: a fast core in C/C++/Rust, with higher-level languages for surrounding functionality.

```mermaid
flowchart TB
    subgraph Hot Path C++ or Rust
        Core[Engine core: state, F, optimization]
    end
    subgraph Warm Path Java, Go, or C#
        App[Application logic: API, business rules]
    end
    subgraph Cold Path Python or JavaScript
        Tools[Tooling: data analysis, ML training, scripts]
    end
    Core -->|called via FFI or RPC| App
    App -->|invokes scripts| Tools
```

This pattern is common:
- **Chess engines:** C++ core; Python scripts for testing and analysis.
- **Search engines:** C++ index and query engine; Java/Go API tier; Python for ML training.
- **Trading engines:** C++ core for the hot path; Java/Python for risk monitoring and reporting.
- **Recommendation engines:** C++ inference engine; Python for model training; Go/Java for the API tier.

The hot path is in the fastest language; the cold path is in the most productive language. The boundary is well-defined (FFI or RPC), so the hot path can be optimized independently.

---

## 7.1.6 Common Pitfalls

### Pitfall 1: Using a Slow Language for the Core

Python, JavaScript, and other interpreted languages are too slow for engine cores. Even with JIT compilation, they cannot match C++/Rust for latency-critical workloads.

### Pitfall 2: Over-Using C++

C++ is appropriate for the engine core, but using it for the API tier, tooling, and ML training is often less productive than using a higher-level language. Use the right language for each layer.

### Pitfall 3: Not Learning Modern C++

C++98 idioms (raw pointers, manual memory management) are error-prone. Modern C++ (smart pointers, RAII, move semantics) is much safer. Learn and use modern C++.

### Pitfall 4: Fighting Rust's Borrow Checker

Engineers new to Rust often fight the borrow checker, trying to write C++-style code in Rust. The result is frustration. Learn Rust's idioms; design code around ownership, not against it.

### Pitfall 5: Mixing Too Many Languages

Each language adds complexity (build systems, FFI, deployment). Use at most 2–3 languages per project.

### Pitfall 6: Not Considering the Team's Expertise

A language the team does not know is a poor choice, even if it is technically superior. Training is a real cost.

### Pitfall 7: Premature Optimization of Language Choice

Do not choose C++/Rust for a prototype that may not need to be fast. Start with a productive language; rewrite the hot path in C++/Rust if needed.

---

## 7.1.7 Important Reminders

- **C, C++, and Rust are the engine languages.** Use them for the core.
- **Modern C++ (C++17+) is much safer than C++98.** Use smart pointers, RAII, move semantics.
- **Rust provides C++-level performance with memory safety.** Recommended for greenfield projects.
- **Avoid Python, JavaScript, and Java for the engine core.** They are too slow or have GC pauses.
- **Use the hybrid approach:** fast core in C++/Rust, productive languages for surrounding functionality.
- **Match the language to the team's expertise.** Training is a real cost.
- **Do not fight the language.** Learn the idioms; design code around them.
- **Premature language optimization is wasteful.** Start productive; rewrite the hot path if needed.

---

## 7.1.8 Summary

C, C++, and Rust are the languages for engine cores. They provide the memory control, hardware access, and zero-cost abstractions that engines require. C++ is the established choice; Rust is the modern alternative with strong safety guarantees.

Other languages (Python, JavaScript, Java, Go, C#) are unsuitable for engine cores due to interpretation overhead, garbage collection, or lack of memory control. They are appropriate for surrounding functionality (API tier, tooling, ML training).

The hybrid approach — fast core in C++/Rust, productive languages for the rest — is the dominant pattern in modern engine engineering.

---

**Next note:** [[2. Memory and Data Structure Optimization Libraries]]

# 5. Zero-Copy Data Pipelines and Kernel Bypass Frameworks

> "For ultra-low-latency engines, the kernel is the enemy. Every syscall costs ~300 ns; every context switch costs ~1000 ns; every packet copy costs ~10 ns. Kernel bypass frameworks eliminate these costs by reading packets directly from the NIC into user-space memory — and the engine's end-to-end latency drops from microseconds to hundreds of nanoseconds."

This is the fifth note in Chapter 7. The previous notes covered languages, data structures, SIMD, and concurrency; this note covers the networking and I/O frameworks that enable ultra-low-latency engines.

---

## 7.5.1 Ultra-Low Latency I/O Systems

### DPDK (Data Plane Development Kit)

**DPDK** is the leading kernel bypass framework for high-speed packet processing. It is used in networking equipment, telecom infrastructure, and high-frequency trading.

**How it works:**
- The NIC is taken out of the kernel's control and put into "user-space" mode.
- Packets are received directly into user-space memory rings, bypassing the kernel network stack entirely.
- The application polls the rings in a busy loop, processing packets with no syscall overhead.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    NIC[Network Interface Card] -->|DPDK: bypass kernel| UR[User-space Rx Ring]
    UR -->|poll| APP[Application]
    APP -->|DPDK: bypass kernel| UT[User-space Tx Ring]
    UT -->|send| NIC
    style NIC fill:#9f9
    style APP fill:#9f9
```

**Performance:**
- End-to-end latency: ~500 ns (vs. ~5 μs with the kernel network stack).
- Throughput: 10+ million packets per second per core.
- CPU usage: 100% per core (busy-polling).

**Use cases:**
- High-frequency trading (read market data, send orders).
- Software routers and switches.
- NFV (Network Function Virtualization).
- Packet capture and analysis.

**Supported NICs:** Intel, Mellanox, Broadcom, and many others. Check the DPDK compatibility list.

### Solarflare OpenOnload

**Solarflare OpenOnload** is a user-space TCP/IP stack for Solarflare NICs. It accelerates the POSIX socket API without requiring application changes.

**How it works:**
- The application uses standard `socket()`, `recv()`, `send()` calls.
- OpenOnload intercepts these calls and routes them through a user-space TCP/IP stack.
- The kernel network stack is bypassed for supported operations.
- Falls back to the kernel for unsupported operations.

**Performance:**
- Latency: ~1 μs improvement over the kernel stack.
- No application changes required.
- POSIX-compatible.

**Use cases:**
- High-frequency trading (FIX protocol over TCP).
- Low-latency financial messaging.
- Any application using POSIX sockets that needs lower latency.

**Limitations:** requires Solarflare NICs; commercial product.

### Linux AF_XDP

**AF_XDP** is Linux's native kernel bypass technology, introduced in Linux 4.18 (2018). It is the open-source alternative to DPDK.

**How it works:**
- The application opens an `AF_XDP` socket and binds it to a NIC.
- Packets matching a filter (set by an eBPF program) are redirected to the socket's user-space ring.
- The application polls the ring, processing packets.

**Performance:**
- Comparable to DPDK for many workloads.
- No proprietary NIC drivers required (works with any NIC supported by Linux).
- Lower CPU usage than DPDK (kernel can handle non-XDP traffic normally).

**Use cases:**
- Similar to DPDK: HFT, packet processing, NFV.
- When you want to avoid DPDK's complexity.
- When you need to mix XDP and normal kernel traffic.

### io_uring (Linux 5.1+)

**io_uring** is Linux's modern asynchronous I/O interface. It is not strictly kernel bypass — the kernel is still involved — but it eliminates the syscall overhead of traditional async I/O (`epoll`, `aio`).

**How it works:**
- The application and kernel share two ring buffers: a submission queue (SQ) and a completion queue (CQ).
- The application submits I/O requests by adding entries to the SQ (no syscall).
- The kernel processes the requests and adds completion entries to the CQ.
- The application polls the CQ for completions (no syscall).
- Syscalls are needed only to "kick" the kernel when SQ entries are not being processed fast enough.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    APP[Application] -->|submit I/O: no syscall| SQ[Submission Queue]
    SQ -->|kernel polls| KERN[Kernel processes]
    KERN -->|completion: no syscall| CQ[Completion Queue]
    CQ -->|poll| APP
```

**Performance:**
- 5–10× higher throughput than `epoll` for high-IOPS workloads.
- Near-zero syscall overhead.
- Supports network I/O, file I/O, timers, and more.

**Use cases:**
- High-throughput network servers (databases, message queues).
- File I/O-intensive applications (databases, log processors).
- When you need async I/O without kernel bypass complexity.

**Libraries:**
- **`liburing`** — the official C library.
- **`tokio-uring`** — Rust bindings for Tokio.
- **`rio`** — Rust io_uring library.

### RDMA (Remote Direct Memory Access)

**RDMA** allows one computer to read/write another computer's memory without involving either CPU. It is the lowest-latency inter-machine communication technology.

**How it works:**
- RDMA NICs (RNICs) can directly access host memory.
- An application on machine A can request the RNIC to read/write a memory region on machine B.
- The RNIC on machine A sends a request to the RNIC on machine B, which performs the read/write.
- Neither CPU is involved.

**Performance:**
- Latency: ~1–2 μs for small messages.
- Throughput: 100+ Gbps on modern RNICs.
- Zero CPU overhead on the remote machine.

**Use cases:**
- Distributed databases (e.g., Teradata, Exadata).
- HPC (High-Performance Computing) clusters.
- Distributed storage (e.g., NVMe-oF).
- High-frequency trading (inter-shard communication).

**Libraries:**
- **`libibverbs`** — the standard RDMA programming library.
- **`librdmacm`** — RDMA connection manager.
- **`rsocket`** — POSIX-like socket API over RDMA.

### Netty (Java)

**Netty** is the leading Java framework for high-performance network applications. It is not kernel bypass, but it provides efficient I/O on top of Java's NIO.

**Features:**
- **Event-driven model.** Netty uses an event loop (Reactor pattern) to handle I/O.
- **Zero-copy.** Byte buffers can be transferred without copying.
- **Channel pipeline.** Modular processing of incoming/outgoing data.
- **SSL/TLS.** Built-in support.
- **HTTP/2, WebSocket.** Protocol implementations.

**Use cases:**
- High-throughput Java servers (Cassandra, Spark, Akka).
- Microservices frameworks (gRPC-Java, Spring WebFlux).
- When you need high-performance networking in Java.

**Performance:** 80–90% of native C/C++ performance for most workloads; sufficient for many engines but not for HFT.

---

## 7.5.2 Comparison of I/O Frameworks

| Framework | Language | Latency | Use Case |
|---|---|---|---|
| Kernel sockets (TCP) | Any | ~5 μs | General-purpose networking |
| OpenOnload | C/C++ (POSIX) | ~1 μs | HFT with POSIX compatibility |
| DPDK | C/C++ | ~500 ns | Maximum performance; HFT, NFV |
| AF_XDP | C/C++ | ~500 ns | Open-source kernel bypass |
| io_uring | C/C++, Rust | ~1 μs | High-throughput async I/O |
| RDMA | C/C++ | ~1–2 μs | Inter-machine communication |
| Netty | Java | ~10 μs | Java high-performance networking |

**For HFT:** DPDK or AF_XDP for the hot path; OpenOnload if POSIX compatibility is required.
**For high-throughput servers:** io_uring (Linux) or Boost.Asio (cross-platform).
**For Java engines:** Netty.
**For distributed engines:** RDMA for inter-machine communication.

---

## 7.5.3 Zero-Copy Techniques

Beyond kernel bypass, several techniques minimize or eliminate data copies:

### `sendfile` and `splice` (Linux)

**`sendfile(out_fd, in_fd, offset, count)`** copies data directly from one file descriptor to another, in the kernel, without copying to user space.

**`splice(fd_in, off_in, fd_out, off_out, len, flags)`** is a more general version that works with pipes.

**Use case:** serving static files (web server), proxying data between sockets.

### Memory-Mapped Files (`mmap`)

**`mmap`** maps a file into the process's address space. Reading from the mapped region reads the file (via the page cache); no explicit `read()` is needed.

```c
void* data = mmap(NULL, file_size, PROT_READ, MAP_PRIVATE, fd, 0);
// data now points to the file contents; no copy
```

**Use case:** reading large read-only data files (indexes, models, lookup tables).

### `writev` and `readv` (Scatter/Gather I/O)

**`writev(fd, iov, iovcnt)`** writes from multiple buffers in a single syscall. **`readv`** reads into multiple buffers.

**Use case:** combining header and body data without copying them into a single buffer.

### Reference-Counted Buffers

Pass buffers by reference (with a reference count) instead of copying. The buffer is freed only when all references are dropped.

**Libraries:**
- **`std::shared_ptr<T>`** (C++) — reference-counted pointer.
- **`bytes::Bytes`** (Rust) — reference-counted byte buffer.
- **`folly::IOBuf`** (C++) — chained buffer with reference counting.
- **`ByteBuf`** (Netty) — reference-counted byte buffer.

### Slice-Based APIs

Functions take slices (`(pointer, length)` pairs) instead of owning containers (`std::string`, `std::vector`). The function does not own the data; it just reads/writes through the slice.

**Libraries:**
- **`std::string_view` / `std::span`** (C++17/C++20).
- **`&[u8]`** (Rust slices).

---

## 7.5.4 The Pipeline Pattern for Zero-Copy

For maximum performance, the entire data pipeline should be zero-copy: from NIC to engine to output, no copies.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    NIC[Network packet] -->|DPDK| RX[User-space Rx ring]
    RX -->|pointer only| PARSE[Parser: pointers into packet]
    PARSE -->|pointer only| ENGINE[Engine core: process]
    ENGINE -->|pointer only| ENC[Encoder: build output]
    ENC -->|pointer only| TX[User-space Tx ring]
    TX -->|DPDK| NIC2[Network packet out]
```

At every stage, data is referenced by pointer, not copied. The packet buffer is allocated once (by the NIC's DMA) and freed once (after the engine is done with it).

This pattern is essential for HFT (where each copy adds nanoseconds) and useful for any high-throughput engine (where each copy adds CPU and memory bandwidth overhead).

---

## 7.5.5 Common Pitfalls

### Pitfall 1: Using Kernel Sockets for HFT

Kernel sockets add ~5 μs of latency — too much for HFT. Use DPDK, AF_XDP, or OpenOnload.

### Pitfall 2: Copying Data Between Layers

Every copy adds latency and CPU overhead. Use pointer-based APIs (slices, IOBuf) to avoid copies.

### Pitfall 3: Not Using `mmap` for Large Read-Only Data

Loading a 10 GB index into memory with `read()` is slow (and copies from kernel to user space). `mmap` is faster and uses less memory (the page cache is shared).

### Pitfall 4: Blocking I/O in the Hot Loop

Blocking I/O stops the engine. Use async I/O (io_uring, Boost.Asio, Tokio) or busy-polling (DPDK).

### Pitfall 5: Not Pinning the NIC's IRQs

By default, NIC interrupts are distributed across all cores. This causes cache thrashing and unpredictable latency. Pin the NIC's IRQs to dedicated cores (`/proc/irq/<irq>/smp_affinity`).

### Pitfall 6: Not Tuning the NIC

Default NIC settings are optimized for throughput, not latency. Tune:
- Disable interrupt coalescing (latency vs. throughput trade-off).
- Increase ring buffer sizes (reduces packet drops under burst).
- Enable RSS (Receive Side Scaling) for multi-core parallelism.

### Pitfall 7: Not Using Huge Pages

Memory-mapped regions and large allocations should use huge pages (2 MB or 1 GB) to reduce TLB misses. Enable with `madvise(MADV_HUGEPAGE)` or mount a hugetlbfs filesystem.

### Pitfall 8: Not Considering Power Management

CPU power management (frequency scaling, C-states) can cause latency spikes. Disable for HFT:
- Set the CPU governor to `performance`.
- Disable C-states deeper than C1.
- Disable turbo boost (it can cause frequency changes mid-execution).

### Pitfall 9: Using TCP When UDP Would Suffice

TCP has overhead (ACKs, retransmission, ordering). For latency-critical one-shot messages, UDP (or RDMA) may be faster.

### Pitfall 10: Not Measuring End-to-End Latency

Latency at each stage does not matter; end-to-end latency does. Measure from packet arrival to packet departure; optimize the longest segment.

---

## 7.5.6 Important Reminders

- **For HFT, use DPDK, AF_XDP, or OpenOnload.** Kernel sockets are too slow.
- **For high-throughput servers, use io_uring (Linux).** Near-zero syscall overhead.
- **For inter-machine communication, use RDMA.** Lowest latency, zero CPU overhead.
- **For Java, use Netty.** Best Java networking framework.
- **Use zero-copy techniques: `mmap`, `sendfile`, `writev`, reference-counted buffers, slices.**
- **Avoid copying data between layers.** Use pointer-based APIs.
- **Pin NIC IRQs to dedicated cores.** Avoid cache thrashing.
- **Tune the NIC: disable interrupt coalescing, increase ring buffers, enable RSS.**
- **Use huge pages for large allocations.** Reduce TLB misses.
- **Disable CPU power management for HFT.** Performance governor, disable deep C-states.
- **Measure end-to-end latency.** Optimize the longest segment.

---

## 7.5.7 Summary

Zero-copy data pipelines and kernel bypass frameworks are essential for ultra-low-latency engines. DPDK, AF_XDP, and OpenOnload bypass the kernel network stack, reducing packet-to-application latency from microseconds to hundreds of nanoseconds. io_uring provides near-zero-syscall async I/O for high-throughput servers. RDMA enables zero-CPU inter-machine communication.

Zero-copy techniques — `mmap`, `sendfile`, `writev`, reference-counted buffers, slice-based APIs — minimize copies throughout the pipeline. The goal: data flows from NIC to engine to output by pointer, never by copy.

Common pitfalls include using kernel sockets for HFT, copying data between layers, not using `mmap` for large read-only data, blocking I/O in the hot loop, not pinning NIC IRQs, not tuning the NIC, not using huge pages, not considering power management, using TCP when UDP would suffice, and not measuring end-to-end latency.

With the right I/O framework and zero-copy techniques, the engine's I/O subsystem can keep up with the engine's compute subsystem — neither is the bottleneck.

---

**Next note:** [[6. Real-World Reference Architectures to Study]]

chapter: 7

tags:

# 4. Thread Schedulers and Concurrency Frameworks

> "Multi-threaded engine code is hard — much harder than single-threaded code. The libraries in this note handle the hard parts (event loops, task graphs, lock-free data structures) so you can focus on the engine logic, not the synchronization primitives."

This is the fourth note in Chapter 7. The previous notes covered languages, data structures, and SIMD; this note covers the concurrency frameworks that enable multi-threaded engines.

---

## 7.4.1 Asynchronous Execution Runtimes

### Tokio (Rust)

**Tokio** is the dominant async runtime for Rust. It provides an event-driven execution model with work-stealing schedulers, async I/O, and timers.

**Features:**
- **Work-stealing scheduler.** Each worker thread has a local queue; idle threads steal work from busy threads. Provides good load balancing.
- **Async I/O.** Built on `epoll` (Linux), `kqueue` (BSD), `IOCP` (Windows). Non-blocking network and file I/O.
- **Timers.** Async sleep, timeouts.
- **Channels.** Multi-producer multi-consumer channels for inter-task communication.

**Usage:**
```rust
use tokio;

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() {
    let listener = tokio::net::TcpListener::bind("0.0.0.0:8080").await?;
    loop {
        let (socket, _) = listener.accept().await?;
        tokio::spawn(handle_connection(socket));
    }
}

async fn handle_connection(mut socket: tokio::net::TcpStream) {
    // ... handle the connection ...
}
```

**When to use:** for any Rust async I/O application. Tokio is the de facto standard.

**Performance:** comparable to hand-written C event loops; faster than most other languages' async runtimes.

### libuv (C, Cross-Platform)

**libuv** is the async I/O library originally developed for Node.js. It is now used by many C and C++ applications.

**Features:**
- **Event loop.** Single-threaded event loop with non-blocking I/O.
- **Thread pool.** For operations that cannot be made async (e.g., file I/O on most OSes).
- **Cross-platform.** Linux, macOS, Windows, BSD.
- **TCP/UDP, pipes, TTY, files, DNS.**

**Usage:**
```c
#include <uv.h>

void on_connect(uv_stream_t* server, int status) {
    // ... handle new connection ...
}

int main() {
    uv_loop_t* loop = uv_default_loop();
    uv_tcp_t server;
    uv_tcp_init(loop, &server);
    
    struct sockaddr_in addr;
    uv_ip4_addr("0.0.0.0", 8080, &addr);
    uv_tcp_bind(&server, (const struct sockaddr*)&addr, 0);
    uv_listen((uv_stream_t*)&server, 128, on_connect);
    
    return uv_run(loop, UV_RUN_DEFAULT);
}
```

**When to use:** for C/C++ applications that need a portable async I/O library. Particularly useful when portability across Linux/macOS/Windows is required.

### Boost.Asio (C++)

**Boost.Asio** is the standard C++ async I/O library. It is the foundation of many C++ network servers.

**Features:**
- **Async I/O.** TCP, UDP, timers, files (on some platforms).
- **Proactor pattern.** Async operations initiate a read/write; a callback is called when complete.
- **Coroutines (C++20).** Can be used with C++20 coroutines for cleaner async code.
- **Buffer management.** `boost::asio::buffer` for safe buffer handling.
- **SSL/TLS.** Built-in SSL support via OpenSSL.

**Usage:**
```cpp
#include <boost/asio.hpp>
using boost::asio::ip::tcp;

void handle_accept(tcp::socket* socket, const boost::system::error_code& ec) {
    // ... handle the connection ...
}

int main() {
    boost::asio::io_context io;
    tcp::acceptor acceptor(io, tcp::endpoint(tcp::v4(), 8080));
    
    tcp::socket* socket = new tcp::socket(io);
    acceptor.async_accept(*socket, std::bind(handle_accept, socket, std::placeholders::_1));
    
    io.run();
}
```

**When to use:** for C++ applications that need async I/O. Boost.Asio is mature, portable, and integrates well with the C++ ecosystem.

### Comparison

| Runtime | Language | Platform | Use Case |
|---|---|---|---|
| Tokio | Rust | Linux, macOS, Windows | Rust async I/O applications |
| libuv | C | Cross-platform | C/C++ applications needing portable async I/O |
| Boost.Asio | C++ | Cross-platform | C++ applications needing async I/O |

For **Rust** engines, use Tokio. For **C++** engines, use Boost.Asio. For **C** engines or projects needing maximum portability, use libuv.

---

## 7.4.2 Task-Graph Scheduling Engines

For CPU-bound multi-threaded workloads, task-graph schedulers provide better performance than raw threads.

### Intel TBB (Threading Building Blocks)

**Intel TBB** is a C++ library for task-based parallelism. It provides a work-stealing scheduler, concurrent containers, and high-level parallel patterns.

**Features:**
- **Work-stealing scheduler.** Each worker thread has a local deque of tasks; idle threads steal from the back of other threads' deques.
- **Parallel patterns.** `parallel_for`, `parallel_reduce`, `parallel_pipeline`, `parallel_sort`. High-level abstractions over the scheduler.
- **Concurrent containers.** `concurrent_hash_map`, `concurrent_vector`, `concurrent_queue`.
- **Flow graph.** A graph-based programming model where nodes are computations and edges are data dependencies.

**Usage:**
```cpp
#include <tbb/parallel_for.h>
#include <tbb/blocked_range.h>

void process(float* data, int n) {
    tbb::parallel_for(tbb::blocked_range<int>(0, n),
        [&](const tbb::blocked_range<int>& r) {
            for (int i = r.begin(); i < r.end(); i++) {
                data[i] = compute(data[i]);
            }
        });
}
```

**When to use:** for CPU-bound C++ applications that need parallelism. TBB is particularly good for data-parallel workloads (process N elements in parallel).

### OpenMP

**OpenMP** is a multi-platform shared-memory parallel programming standard. It uses compiler pragmas to express parallelism.

**Features:**
- **Pragmas.** `#pragma omp parallel for` parallelizes the next loop.
- **Tasks.** `#pragma omp task` creates a task.
- **Reductions.** `#pragma omp parallel for reduction(+:sum)` parallelizes a loop with a sum reduction.
- **SIMD.** `#pragma omp simd` forces SIMD vectorization.

**Usage:**
```c
#include <omp.h>

void process(float* data, int n) {
    #pragma omp parallel for
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        data[i] = compute(data[i]);
    }
}

float sum(float* data, int n) {
    float result = 0;
    #pragma omp parallel for reduction(+:result)
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        result += data[i];
    }
    return result;
}
```

**When to use:** for quick parallelization of existing C/C++ loops. OpenMP is simpler than TBB for basic parallelism, but less flexible.

### Rayon (Rust)

**Rayon** is a Rust library for data parallelism, similar to TBB but with Rust's safety guarantees.

**Features:**
- **Work-stealing scheduler.** Like TBB.
- **Parallel iterators.** `par_iter()` instead of `iter()` for parallel iteration.
- **Scoped threads.** Safely borrow data across threads.

**Usage:**
```rust
use rayon::prelude::*;

fn process(data: &mut [f32]) {
    data.par_iter_mut().for_each(|x| {
        *x = compute(*x);
    });
}

fn sum(data: &[f32]) -> f32 {
    data.par_iter().sum()
}
```

**When to use:** for any Rust application that needs data parallelism. Rayon is the Rust equivalent of TBB, with better safety guarantees.

### Comparison

| Library | Language | Model | Use Case |
|---|---|---|---|
| Intel TBB | C++ | Task graph | CPU-bound C++ parallelism |
| OpenMP | C, C++, Fortran | Pragmas | Quick parallelization of existing loops |
| Rayon | Rust | Parallel iterators | Rust data parallelism |

For **C++** engines, use TBB for sophisticated parallelism, OpenMP for quick parallelization. For **Rust** engines, use Rayon.

---

## 7.4.3 Lock-Free Data Structures

For multi-threaded engines, lock-free data structures avoid the overhead and contention of mutexes.

### Boost.Lockfree

**Boost.Lockfree** provides lock-free data structures in C++:

- **`boost::lockfree::queue`.** Multi-producer multi-consumer queue.
- **`boost::lockfree::stack`.** Multi-producer multi-consumer stack.
- **`boost::lockfree::spsc_queue`.** Single-producer single-consumer queue (fastest; no atomic operations needed for the queue itself).

**Usage:**
```cpp
#include <boost/lockfree/queue.hpp>

boost::lockfree::queue<int> q(1024);

// Producer thread
q.push(42);

// Consumer thread
int value;
while (!q.pop(value)) {
    // spin or sleep
}
```

### Folly Lock-Free Structures

**Folly** provides several lock-free structures:

- **`folly::MPMCQueue`.** Multi-producer multi-consumer queue.
- **`folly::AtomicHashMap`.** Fixed-size concurrent hash map.
- **`folly::ConcurrentHashMap`.** Growable concurrent hash map.
- **`folly::HazPtr`.** Hazard pointer implementation for safe memory reclamation in lock-free code.

### Crossbeam (Rust)

**Crossbeam** is the Rust library for lock-free data structures:

- **`crossbeam::queue::ArrayQueue` / `SegQueue`.** Multi-producer multi-consumer queues.
- **`crossbeam::channel`.** Multi-producer multi-consumer channels.
- **`crossbeam::epoch`.** Epoch-based memory reclamation for lock-free code.
- **`crossbeam::deque`.** Work-stealing deques.

### The Disruptor Pattern

The **Disruptor** pattern (Martin Thompson, LMAX) is a single-producer single-consumer ring buffer with no locks. It is the canonical pattern for HFT event processing.

**Implementation:**
```c
struct alignas(64) RingBuffer {
    Cell cells[SIZE];  // power-of-2 size
    alignas(64) std::atomic<uint64_t> cursor;
    alignas(64) std::atomic<uint64_t> consumer_cursor;
};

void publish(RingBuffer* rb, const Cell* cell) {
    uint64_t pos = rb->cursor.load(std::memory_order_relaxed);
    rb->cells[pos & (SIZE - 1)] = *cell;
    rb->cursor.store(pos + 1, std::memory_order_release);
}

bool consume(RingBuffer* rb, Cell* out) {
    uint64_t pos = rb->consumer_cursor.load(std::memory_order_relaxed);
    if (pos >= rb->cursor.load(std::memory_order_acquire)) {
        return false;  // empty
    }
    *out = rb->cells[pos & (SIZE - 1)];
    rb->consumer_cursor.store(pos + 1, std::memory_order_relaxed);
    return true;
}
```

The `alignas(64)` ensures the cursor and consumer_cursor are on separate cache lines, avoiding false sharing between the producer and consumer threads.

**Performance:** ~10 ns per event handoff, vs. ~1 μs for a `std::mutex`-protected queue — 100× faster.

---

## 7.4.4 Choosing a Concurrency Framework

The choice depends on the workload:

| Workload | Recommended framework |
|---|---|
| I/O-bound (network server) | Tokio (Rust), Boost.Asio (C++), libuv (C) |
| CPU-bound, data-parallel | Rayon (Rust), TBB (C++), OpenMP (any) |
| CPU-bound, task-graph | TBB (C++) |
| Inter-thread queue | Disruptor pattern, folly::MPMCQueue, crossbeam::queue |
| Concurrent hash map | folly::ConcurrentHashMap, crossbeam-skiplist |
| HFT event loop | Custom Disruptor + busy-polling |

For most engines, the combination of an async runtime (Tokio/Asio) for I/O and a task scheduler (Rayon/TBB) for CPU-bound work provides a good balance.

---

## 7.4.5 Common Pitfalls

### Pitfall 1: Using `std::mutex` in the Hot Path

`std::mutex` is ~20 ns per lock/unlock (uncontended); contended locks can be 1000+ ns. For hot-path code, use lock-free data structures or per-thread data.

### Pitfall 2: False Sharing

Two threads modifying different fields of the same cache line cause the cache line to bounce between cores, killing performance. Pad shared data to cache line boundaries (`alignas(64)`).

### Pitfall 3: Lock Contention

A single lock protecting a hot data structure becomes a bottleneck. Use sharded locks, lock-free structures, or per-thread data.

### Pitfall 4: Not Using Work-Stealing

A simple thread pool with a shared queue has lock contention on the queue. Work-stealing schedulers (TBB, Rayon, Tokio) have per-thread queues, reducing contention.

### Pitfall 5: Blocking the Event Loop

Async runtimes (Tokio, Asio) run on a small number of threads. Blocking one (e.g., with a long computation or a blocking syscall) prevents other tasks from running. Use `spawn_blocking` (Tokio) or a separate thread pool for blocking work.

### Pitfall 6: Not Pinning Threads to Cores

Thread migration loses cache state. Pin threads to specific cores with `pthread_setaffinity_np` (Linux) or `SetThreadAffinityMask` (Windows).

### Pitfall 7: Not Considering NUMA

On multi-socket systems, accessing remote memory is 2× slower than local memory. Use NUMA-aware allocation (`numa_alloc_onnode`).

### Pitfall 8: Too Many Threads

More threads than cores leads to context switching overhead. Use at most 1×–2× the number of cores.

### Pitfall 9: Not Using Hazard Pointers / Epoch-Based Reclamation

Naive lock-free code that frees memory immediately can cause use-after-free bugs. Use hazard pointers (Folly's HazPtr) or epoch-based reclamation (Crossbeam's epoch).

### Pitfall 10: Not Testing Under High Load

Concurrency bugs often appear only under high load. Test with realistic (and unrealistic) load; use tools like `stress-ng`, `wrk`, or `vegeta` for load testing.

---

## 7.4.6 Important Reminders

- **Use async runtimes (Tokio, Asio, libuv) for I/O-bound workloads.** They handle thousands of connections efficiently.
- **Use task schedulers (Rayon, TBB, OpenMP) for CPU-bound workloads.** They provide work-stealing and high-level parallel patterns.
- **Use lock-free data structures (Disruptor, folly::MPMCQueue, crossbeam::queue) for inter-thread communication.** 100× faster than mutex-protected queues.
- **Pad shared data to cache line boundaries.** Avoid false sharing.
- **Pin threads to cores.** Avoid migration.
- **Use NUMA-aware allocation on multi-socket systems.**
- **Do not use more threads than cores.** Context switching overhead dominates.
- **Use hazard pointers or epoch-based reclamation in lock-free code.** Avoid use-after-free.
- **Test under high load.** Concurrency bugs appear only under load.

---

## 7.4.7 Summary

Concurrency frameworks handle the hard parts of multi-threaded programming: event loops, task graphs, lock-free data structures.

For **async I/O**, use Tokio (Rust), Boost.Asio (C++), or libuv (C). For **CPU-bound parallelism**, use Rayon (Rust), TBB (C++), or OpenMP (any). For **inter-thread communication**, use the Disruptor pattern, folly::MPMCQueue, or crossbeam::queue.

Common pitfalls include using `std::mutex` in the hot path, false sharing, lock contention, not using work-stealing, blocking the event loop, not pinning threads, ignoring NUMA, using too many threads, not using hazard pointers, and not testing under load.

With the right concurrency framework, multi-threaded engines can scale to many cores with minimal synchronization overhead.

---

**Previous note:** [[3. Vectorization and SIMD Libraries]]
**Next note:** [[5. Zero-Copy Data Pipelines and Kernel Bypass Frameworks]]

# 3. Vectorization and SIMD Libraries

> "SIMD is the hidden superpower of modern engines. A single AVX-512 instruction processes 16 floats in the same time a scalar instruction processes one — a 16× speedup, for free, if your data is laid out correctly. The libraries in this note make SIMD accessible without requiring you to write intrinsics."

This is the third note in Chapter 7. The previous notes covered languages and data structures; this note covers the libraries that make vectorization practical.

---

## 7.3.1 CPU Intrinsics and Vectorization Toolkits

### Intel Intrinsics (SSE, AVX, AVX-512)

Intel's SIMD instruction sets:

- **SSE (Streaming SIMD Extensions).** 128-bit registers. 4 floats or 2 doubles per instruction. Available on all x86-64 CPUs.
- **AVX / AVX2 (Advanced Vector Extensions).** 256-bit registers. 8 floats or 4 doubles per instruction. Available on most x86-64 CPUs since 2013.
- **AVX-512.** 512-bit registers. 16 floats or 8 doubles per instruction. Available on server x86 since 2017. Has quirks (frequency throttling on some CPUs).

**Intrinsics** are C/C++ functions that map directly to SIMD instructions. They are accessed via `<immintrin.h>` (x86) or `<arm_neon.h>` (ARM).

```c
#include <immintrin.h>

// Add 8 floats using AVX2
__m256 va = _mm256_loadu_ps(a);  // load 8 floats
__m256 vb = _mm256_loadu_ps(b);
__m256 vc = _mm256_add_ps(va, vb);  // add 8 floats in 1 instruction
_mm256_storeu_ps(c, vc);
```

**Pros:**
- Maximum control over code generation.
- Access to the full SIMD instruction set.
- Predictable performance.

**Cons:**
- Architecture-specific (AVX2 code does not run on ARM).
- Hard to read and maintain.
- Easy to introduce bugs (alignment, masking, etc.).

**When to use intrinsics:** only for the hottest loops where libraries are insufficient. For everything else, use libraries or auto-vectorization.

### ARM NEON

ARM's SIMD instruction set, available on all ARMv7+ processors (including all Apple Silicon, all Android phones, all AWS Graviton instances).

- 128-bit registers. 4 floats or 2 doubles per instruction.
- Similar functionality to SSE.

```c
#include <arm_neon.h>

// Add 4 floats using NEON
float32x4_t va = vld1q_f32(a);
float32x4_t vb = vld1q_f32(b);
float32x4_t vc = vaddq_f32(va, vb);
vst1q_f32(c, vc);
```

### SVE (ARM Scalable Vector Extension)

ARM's newer SIMD instruction set, available on ARMv9 and HPC processors. The key innovation: the vector length is **variable** (implemented by the CPU, not the programmer). The same code runs on 128-bit, 256-bit, 512-bit, or 2048-bit CPUs.

SVE is the future of ARM SIMD, but adoption is still limited (mainly HPC and server CPUs).

### SIMDe — Portable SIMD Abstraction

**SIMDe** is a header-only library that translates SIMD intrinsics across architectures. Write AVX2 code; SIMDe translates it to NEON on ARM, or vice versa.

```c
#include <simde/x86/avx2.h>

// This code uses AVX2 syntax but runs on any architecture
__m256 va = simde_mm256_loadu_ps(a);
__m256 vb = simde_mm256_loadu_ps(b);
__m256 vc = simde_mm256_add_ps(va, vb);
simde_mm256_storeu_ps(c, vc);
```

**Pros:**
- Write once, run on any architecture.
- Same code for x86 and ARM.
- No performance overhead (the translations are direct).

**Cons:**
- Does not abstract away differences in available operations (some AVX-512 operations have no NEON equivalent).
- Still uses intrinsic syntax (less readable than libraries).

**When to use SIMDe:** when you need cross-architecture SIMD with intrinsic-level control. For higher-level operations (linear algebra, JSON parsing), use specialized libraries.

### Highway (Google)

**Highway** is Google's portable SIMD library, providing a higher-level abstraction than SIMDe.

```cpp
#include <hwy/highway.h>
namespace hn = hwy::HWY_NAMESPACE;

void Add(const float* a, const float* b, float* c, size_t n) {
    for (size_t i = 0; i < n; i += hn::Lanes(hn::ScalableTag<float>())) {
        auto va = hn::Load(a + i);
        auto vb = hn::Load(b + i);
        auto vc = hn::Add(va, vb);
        hn::Store(vc, c + i);
    }
}
```

**Pros:**
- Higher-level than intrinsics.
- Portable across architectures.
- Supports "scalable" vectors (the vector length is determined at runtime).

**Cons:**
- Less mature than Intel intrinsics or SIMDe.
- Smaller community.

### std::simd (C++26)

The C++ standardization committee is working on `std::simd`, a portable SIMD abstraction in the standard library. It will be available in C++26 (or later).

```cpp
#include <simd>
namespace std::experimental::simd;

std::simd<float> a, b, c;
c = a + b;  // element-wise add
```

Until `std::simd` is widely available, use SIMDe or Highway for portable SIMD.

---

## 7.3.2 High-Performance Linear Algebra

For matrix and vector operations, dedicated linear algebra libraries are 10–100× faster than hand-written code.

### Eigen (C++)

**Eigen** is the dominant C++ linear algebra library. It is header-only (no linking required), expression-template-based (no temporaries), and supports SIMD automatically.

**Features:**
- Vectors, matrices, tensors of arbitrary size.
- All common linear algebra operations (multiply, invert, decompose, solve).
- SIMD auto-vectorization (uses SSE, AVX, NEON automatically).
- Expression templates (no temporaries for chained operations).
- Sparse matrices.
- Geometry module (quaternions, rotations).

**Usage:**
```cpp
#include <Eigen/Dense>
using namespace Eigen;

VectorXf a(1000), b(1000), c(1000);
// ... fill a and b ...
c = a + b;             // element-wise add, SIMD-vectorized
c = a.array() * b.array();  // element-wise multiply

MatrixXf M(100, 100);
VectorXf x(100), y(100);
y = M * x;  // matrix-vector multiply, SIMD-vectorized
```

**Performance:** Eigen uses SIMD automatically. For matrix multiplication, Eigen achieves > 90% of theoretical peak performance on modern CPUs.

**Use cases:**
- Machine learning (training and inference).
- Physics simulation.
- Computer graphics.
- Recommendation engines (similarity computations).

### Blaze (C++)

**Blaze** is another C++ linear algebra library, similar to Eigen but with a different design philosophy. Blaze is more focused on performance and less on generality.

**Pros:**
- Faster than Eigen for some operations (especially sparse matrices).
- Cleaner expression templates.

**Cons:**
- Smaller community than Eigen.
- Less feature-rich.

**Use cases:** same as Eigen; consider Blaze if Eigen's performance is insufficient.

### xtensor (C++)

**xtensor** is a C++ library inspired by NumPy. It provides N-dimensional arrays with NumPy-like syntax.

**Pros:**
- NumPy-like syntax (familiar to Python users).
- Supports SIMD via xtensor-blas.
- Interoperates with Python (via xtensor-python).

**Cons:**
- Slower than Eigen for pure linear algebra.
- Less mature.

**Use cases:** when NumPy-like syntax is important; when interoperating with Python.

### GSL (GNU Scientific Library)

**GSL** is a C library for scientific computing. It provides linear algebra, statistics, optimization, and more.

**Pros:** comprehensive; C interface (callable from many languages).
**Cons:** older; not as fast as Eigen for linear algebra.

---

## 7.3.3 Specialized SIMD Libraries

Beyond general linear algebra, specialized libraries provide SIMD-optimized implementations of specific operations.

### SIMDJSON (JSON Parsing)

**SIMDJSON** is a JSON parser that uses SIMD to achieve 1+ GB/s parsing speed. This is 10–100× faster than standard JSON parsers.

**Usage:**
```cpp
#include "simdjson.h"
using namespace simdjson;

ondemand::parser parser;
auto json = padded_string::load("data.json");
auto doc = parser.iterate(json);
for (auto item : doc["items"]) {
    std::string name = item["name"];
    int price = item["price"];
    // ...
}
```

**When to use:** whenever you parse JSON in the hot path. The speedup is dramatic.

### Hyperscan (Regex Matching)

**Hyperscan** is Intel's regex matching library, achieving 10+ GB/s for many patterns. Uses SIMD and automata-based matching.

**Use cases:**
- Network intrusion detection (Snort, Suricata).
- Log analysis.
- Filter pipelines.

**When to use:** when you need to match many regex patterns at high speed.

### simde (Fallback for Non-x86)

Already covered above. Use SIMDe when you need AVX/SSE code to run on ARM.

### OpenBLAS / MKL (BLAS Implementations)

**OpenBLAS** is an open-source BLAS (Basic Linear Algebra Subprograms) implementation. **MKL** (Intel Math Kernel Library) is Intel's proprietary BLAS, optimized for Intel CPUs.

Both provide highly optimized implementations of matrix multiply, vector operations, etc. They are typically faster than Eigen for large matrices.

**Use cases:**
- Large matrix operations (> 1000×1000).
- When you need BLAS-compatible API (e.g., for use with other scientific libraries).

**Eigen vs OpenBLAS/MKL:** for most engine use cases, Eigen is sufficient. For very large matrices, OpenBLAS or MKL may be faster.

---

## 7.3.4 Auto-Vectorization

Modern compilers (GCC, Clang) can auto-vectorize plain C/C++ loops. The loop must satisfy several conditions (Chapter 4.4).

### Enabling Auto-Vectorization

```bash
# GCC / Clang
g++ -O3 -march=native -fopt-info-vec ./engine.cpp
```

- `-O3` enables optimization level 3, which includes auto-vectorization.
- `-march=native` enables use of the host CPU's SIMD instruction sets.
- `-fopt-info-vec` (GCC) or `-Rpass=loop-vectorize` (Clang) prints which loops were vectorized.

### Helping the Compiler Vectorize

```c
// Use __restrict__ to indicate no aliasing
void add(float* __restrict__ a, float* __restrict__ b, float* __restrict__ c, int n) {
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        c[i] = a[i] + b[i];
    }
}

// Use #pragma omp simd to force vectorization
#pragma omp simd
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
    c[i] = a[i] + b[i];
}

// Use branchless operations
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
    c[i] = std::max(std::min(a[i], hi), lo);  // branchless clamp
}
```

### Verifying Auto-Vectorization

Compile with `-fopt-info-vec` and verify that the hot loops were vectorized. If a loop was not vectorized, the compiler will print a reason (data dependency, aliasing, etc.).

For the truly curious, use Compiler Explorer (godbolt.org) to see the assembly output and verify SIMD instructions are used.

---

## 7.3.5 Common Pitfalls

### Pitfall 1: Hand-Writing Intrinsics When Libraries Suffice

Intrinsics are hard to read, hard to debug, and architecture-specific. Use libraries (Eigen, SIMDJSON, Hyperscan) wherever possible. Reserve intrinsics for the hottest loops where libraries are insufficient.

### Pitfall 2: Not Using Auto-Vectorization

Modern compilers are good at auto-vectorization. Use `-O3 -march=native` and let the compiler do its job. Verify with `-fopt-info-vec`.

### Pitfall 3: Forgetting `__restrict__`

Without `__restrict__`, the compiler must assume pointers alias and cannot vectorize. Always use `__restrict__` for distinct array parameters.

### Pitfall 4: Branches in Vectorized Loops

Branches prevent vectorization (or force expensive masked execution). Use branchless patterns.

### Pitfall 5: Not Using SoA Layout

SIMD requires consecutive memory. AoS layout cannot be vectorized. Always restructure to SoA first.

### Pitfall 6: AVX-512 Frequency Throttling

On some CPUs (Intel Skylake-X), AVX-512 instructions cause frequency throttling for several milliseconds. Benchmark on your target hardware.

### Pitfall 7: Not Benchmarking Different SIMD Widths

AVX-512 is not always faster than AVX2 (due to frequency throttling and lower clock speed). Benchmark both.

### Pitfall 8: Ignoring Alignment

Aligned loads are faster than unaligned. Align data to SIMD width boundaries (32 bytes for AVX2, 64 bytes for AVX-512).

### Pitfall 9: Not Using Libraries for Specialized Operations

For JSON parsing, use SIMDJSON (not a generic parser). For regex, use Hyperscan (not `std::regex`). For linear algebra, use Eigen (not hand-written loops).

---

## 7.3.6 Important Reminders

- **Use libraries (Eigen, SIMDJSON, Hyperscan) wherever possible.** Reserve intrinsics for the hottest loops.
- **Use auto-vectorization (`-O3 -march=native`).** Let the compiler do its job.
- **Use `__restrict__` and `#pragma omp simd` to help vectorization.**
- **Use SoA layout for SIMD-friendly data.**
- **Use branchless patterns in vectorized loops.**
- **Align data to SIMD width boundaries.**
- **Use SIMDe or Highway for cross-architecture portability.**
- **Benchmark different SIMD widths (AVX2 vs AVX-512).**
- **Verify auto-vectorization with `-fopt-info-vec`.**

---

## 7.3.7 Summary

SIMD is the hidden superpower of modern engines, providing 4–16× speedup for data-parallel operations. The libraries in this note make SIMD accessible:

- **Intrinsics (Intel SSE/AVX/AVX-512, ARM NEON/SVE)** for maximum control.
- **SIMDe and Highway** for cross-architecture portability.
- **Eigen, Blaze, xtensor** for linear algebra.
- **SIMDJSON** for JSON parsing (1+ GB/s).
- **Hyperscan** for regex matching (10+ GB/s).
- **OpenBLAS / MKL** for large matrix operations.

Auto-vectorization (`-O3 -march=native`) handles many cases automatically. Help the compiler with `__restrict__`, `#pragma omp simd`, SoA layout, and branchless patterns. Verify vectorization with `-fopt-info-vec`.

Reserve hand-written intrinsics for the hottest loops where libraries are insufficient. Always benchmark; AVX-512 is not always faster than AVX2.

---

**Next note:** [[4. Thread Schedulers and Concurrency Frameworks]]
